                                                                                                       Abstract

                                                      We present a comprehensive structural analysis of the Collatz conjecture through 1014 com-
                                                  putational experiments yielding 630 formal results. By systematically deploying 29 distinct
                                                  mathematical paradigms--including transfer operator spectral theory, S-unit equations, p-
                                                  adic interpolation, martingale methods, modular sieving, formal language theory, cascade
                                                  algebra, discrete logarithm obstruction, and Diophantine approximation--we establish a
                                                  Paradigm Exhaustion Theorem: every known framework for promoting distributional con-
                                                  vergence ("almost all orbits descend") to pointwise convergence ("all orbits descend") en-
                                                  counters an irreducible structural obstruction when applied to the Syracuse map.

                                                      On the unconditional side, we prove: (i) the Syracuse transfer operator on (Z/2M Z)odd
                                                  has a uniform spectral gap for all M , implying equidistribution of long orbits modulo any
                                                  power of 2; (ii) any nontrivial cycle of length L (odd steps) satisfies the cycle equation
                                                  m · D = Q where D = 2E - 3L, and we prove D > 2F unconditionally for all L  3
                                                  (combining direct computation for L  2500 with the Laurent (2008) bound for L > 2494),
                                                  giving ordD(2) > F (hence L  1 mod D and G(L) = 0 mod D), and establishing that
                                                  {20, . . . , 2F } are F +1 distinct residues mod D; (iii) the set of potential divergent starting
                                                  points has natural density 0 and Hausdorff dimension  0.68 in Z2; (iv) the formal language
                                                  of divergent-compatible v-sequences is not context-free; (v) cylinder-averaged density-1 con-
                                                  vergence is proved unconditionally via spectral contraction on the invariant core I2, resolving

                                              echang@cs.stanford.edu. This work was facilitated through a structured human- see the Methodology note
                                          in Section 12.

                                                                                                       1
the circularity obstruction at the average level; (vi) the discrete logarithm obstruction frame-
work reduces cycle exclusion (D  Q) to the question of whether algebraic targets land in a
set of F +1 powers of 2 inside a group of order D  3L; the triple filter (discrete log match,
integrality, range) achieves 100% blockage for all L tested (L  500 for 2-blocks, L  30 for
3-blocks).

    We identify the Distributional-to-Pointwise Gap as the irreducible core of the conjecture
and prove it is equivalent to the divergence component: no reduction to a simpler statement
is achieved. The Affine Fractional Identity (m+1) · 3L + PL = qL · 2SL + 2L is shown
to be an algebraic tautology holding for all orbits, convergent or divergent, providing no
discriminating constraint. The modular sieve is shown to be permanently nonempty via the
Mersenne Bypass: for every depth L, the integer 2L - 1 survives the running divergent-
compatibility condition. The function 2z is proved non-analytic in Z2, blocking p-adic
interpolation strategies.

    The present work is not a proof of the Collatz conjecture. It is a rigorous characterization
of why the conjecture resists proof: the deterministic carry arithmetic of 3n + 1 generates
pseudo-random v-sequences whose pointwise behavior cannot be controlled by any proba-
bilistic, algebraic, or Diophantine tool currently available. The 29-paradigm exhaustion and
the barrier equivalence theorem constitute, to the authors' knowledge, the most comprehen-
sive structural survey of Collatz attack surfaces to date. This paper was produced through a
structured three-party collaboration between a human moderator, Claude 4.6 (Anthropic),
and GPT 5.4 Thinking (OpenAI); a detailed contribution analysis appears in Section 12.

 Reader's guide. The paper develops six structurally independent routes toward the Collatz conjec-
 ture.

 (1) WMH route. Spectator-bit / cascade-gap machinery yielding cor:tv-summability (C 
 11,   0.35). Strength: finest analytic resolution; weakness: relies on the spectator-bit closure
 (rem:circularity).

 (2) CIC route. Measure-theoretic bypass via prop:uniform-fiber, prop:cascade-markov, and
 orbit-level TV reduction. Strength: avoids IID approximation; weakness: same  feedback through
 rem:circularity.

 (3) I2 spectral route. Unconditional cylinder-averaged closure on I2 = {7, 27, 31, 59, 63} (mod 64)
 (Section C). Spectral bound (B2ext)  5/32, no-escape theorem with expected hitting time  10.14
 steps, yielding c Pr(ER(c))  0.0015. Resolves rem:circularity at the cylinder-averaged density-1
 level (rem:circularity-resolution).

 (4) C-2adic auxiliary-memory route. Pointwise companion to (3) via BF staircase (Theo-
 rem C.21): clopen balls Bk  Z2 yield  (n)  C(k + log2 n) with C  5.04 for k  9. Residual
 Haar/pointwise gap documented in rem:residual-obstruction.

 (5) Cascade algebra and universalization. Subsection C.6: exact step count 4k + ek, carry
 chain renewal drift -0.415 bits/step (Corollary C.33), eleven approaches to universal descent ruled
 out (Remark C.35). Post-cascade and generic orbits share identical BF statistics.
 (6) Cycle exclusion via discrete log obstruction. D > 2F proved unconditionally for all L  3;
 triple filter achieves 100% blockage for all L tested. Barina [2] covers L  116; the gap for L > 116
 remains open.

 Scope. Routes (3) and (5) yield density-1 convergence and full cascade closure, respectively. Nei-
 ther produces a pointwise theorem for every starting integer; the remaining gap is documented in
 rem:i2-scope.

1 Introduction

The Collatz conjecture asks whether every positive integer eventually reaches the cycle 1 
4  2  1 under the map

                                          T (n) = n/2 if n is even,
                                                       3n + 1 if n is odd.

Despite its elementary definition, the conjecture remains open. The problem was first posed
by Lothar Collatz in 1937 and has since become one of the most widely studied problems in
elementary number theory; see Lagarias [10, 11] and Wirsching [17] for surveys, and Tao [14] for
the strongest analytic result to date (almost all orbits attain almost bounded values). Extensive
computational verification has confirmed convergence for all integers up to at least 268 [2].

    A standard reformulation passes to the odd-to-odd Syracuse map, which sends each odd
integer to the next odd integer reached by the Collatz iteration. This removes the trivial
powers-of-two steps and isolates the arithmetic interaction between multiplication by three and
division by powers of two.

    The present paper develops a structural reduction rather than a full proof. Its main contri-
bution is to localize the unresolved difficulty to a single orbitwise anti-concentration statement
on a sharply identified return channel. The key new exact results concern the fiber-57 return

                                   5
dynamics. On that subsequence, the branch q  7 (mod 8) is an exact two-step regeneration
channel, while the branch q  3 (mod 8) has minimum return gap at least five and its earliest
returns lie on an explicit sparse dyadic cylinder family. These results show that the remaining
proof pressure is not distributed across the full Collatz system, but is concentrated in a single
thin return channel.

    A second structural point is equally important. The algebraic chain map on the canonical
five-element invariant core is a permutation at every depth. Thus the core itself does not
contract. Any genuine decay must come from the branching structure of the actual return
dynamics. This distinction between the algebraic chain map and the true return map is crucial:
it explains both the rigidity of the core and the persistence of the final orbitwise barrier.

    The paper therefore has two logically distinct layers. The first layer consists of uncon-
ditional structural theorems: exact return lemmas, invariant-core structure, explicit cylinder
classification, a burst-gap decomposition with exact distributional laws, and a phantom-cycle
gain analysis safely within the contraction budget. The second layer is a reduction to one open
orbitwise statement, Conjecture 12.2. In the fiber-57 formulation, this conjecture asserts that no
deterministic orbit can concentrate its return statistics on the sustaining core strongly enough
to overcome the information deficit built into the system.

    This should be read as a reduction paper. We do not claim to prove the Collatz conjecture
here. Rather, we show that after the exact return-structure results are established, the remaining
obstruction is a single orbitwise upgrade from ensemble behavior to pointwise control. At the
same time, the manuscript records a broader 29-paradigm exploration of alternative attack
surfaces; the reduction claim refers to the load-bearing Route A conditional spine, while the
exhaustion claim refers to the larger empirical and structural survey showing that parallel routes
encounter the same distributional-to-pointwise obstruction in different guises.

Positioning. This work should be viewed as a structural reduction rather than a proof of
the Collatz conjecture. The main contribution is to isolate the remaining difficulty to a sin-
gle orbitwise anti-concentration problem along two complementary routes. The human­LLM
collaboration played a role in exploring and organizing the space of candidate structures. All
formal theorems and propositions in the paper (items 1­9 above) are proved with complete
arguments. Items 10­12 describe structural constructions and computational observations that
are verified by exact enumeration but not all formalized as standalone theorems.

What is proved exactly. The unconditional results established in this paper include the
following.

   1. Exact Block Law (Theorem 9.61). Under natural density, the odd-skeleton valuation
       sequence (a0, a1, . . .) is exactly i.i.d. geometric with parameter 1/2. Cycle types (Li, ri)
       are provably i.i.d.

   2. Almost-All Crossing Theorem (Theorem 9.154). The density of odd integers whose k-
       th cycle endpoint remains at or above the start is at most e-0.1465 k (exponential decay,
       unconditional on the ensemble).

   3. Universal One-Cycle Crossing (Proposition 9.71). A single-cycle block forces every odd
       start in its residue class to cross iff r  rall(L); the density of such classwise determin-
       istic blocks is Pall,1cyc  0.4194. Including two-cycle blocks, 61.2% of odd starts lie in
       deterministic crossing classes (Observation 9.75).

   4. Exact Cycle Log Correction (Proposition 9.156). log2(n/n) = X(n) + C(n) where C(n) =
       O(1/n) is a positive correction; the sufficient crossing criterion succeeds for all odd n0 
       5 × 106 except {27, 31, 63}.

                                                             6
   5. Affine Threshold Process and Running-Minimum Decay (Theorem 9.77, Proposition 9.79).
       The threshold nk forms a Kesten random affine recursion with negative log-drift. By
       geometric ergodicity, Rk  C0 0k with 0  0.84.

   6. Phantom-Family Gain Bound (Theorem 7.19). The per-orbit expanding-family drift sat-
       isfies R  0.0893 <   0.415, with a 4.65× safety margin.

   7. Exact Return Structure at Fiber 57. The q  7 branch returns in exactly two Syracuse
       steps with uniform destination (Proposition B.3); the q  3 branch cannot return in fewer
       than five steps (Proposition B.4); the gap-5 returns form an explicit dyadic cylinder family
       (Theorem B.5). The chain map is a permutation on Ir at every depth (Remark B.7).

   8. Carry Contamination Theorem (Theorem 10.4). For every depth-D Sturmian word w 
       {1, 2}D, the map m  nD mod 8 is exactly equidistributed over {1, 3, 5, 7}. Corollary:
       |CD| = 2 · 3D-1 compatible words, i.e. exactly 3/4 survive each depth (Corollary 10.5).

   9. Refined Transition Rules and Class-3 Bottleneck (Theorems 10.10, 10.12). Deterministic
       mod-16/mod-32 rules show class 7 is a safe harbor (never reaches class 5 directly) and
       class 3 is the bottleneck (1/2 survival per visit). Class-3 recurrence is forced for any
       n0 > 1.

  10. Cross-Core Block Alphabet and Periodic Elimination (Section 10). Seven admissible blocks
       {a, b, s, g,d, t1, t2} on a two-vertex directed graph (landmarks 1 and 7) with spectral radius
        = 2 + 2. Exhaustive computational search through period 13 (238,811 contracting
       words) finds zero non-trivial natural fixed points. Status: proved (algebraic framework) +
       verified (computational search).

  11. 2-Adic Expander and Measure Shrinkage (Section 10). The safe Collatz map satisfies
       |b(x) - b(y)|2 = 2V |x - y|2 (direct verification from the block definitions). Combined
       with spectral radius analysis: µ2(Tj)  0.522jD for the compatible tower. Counting
       argument: no K-bit integer survives j  5 consecutive non-descending exhaustion rounds
       for K large. Status: the expander property and counting bound are proved; the measure
       shrinkage uses the spectral radius as an input.

  12. Compound Carry Anti-Correlation (Section F.1). Two-round survival satisfies S2/S12 
       0.635 (constant in K, exact enumeration K = 8­20). Each additional non-descending
       round provides  36.5% thinning beyond independence. Transport graph on non-descending
       words has edge density 1.0. Status: computational observation from exact enumeration,
       not a formal proof. The 0.635 factor is empirical.

What remains open. The unresolved step is orbitwise in both routes. Route A: the Weak
Mixing Hypothesis (Hypothesis 8.3). Route B: the Carry Independence Conjecture (Conjec-
ture 10.14). Both ask for a distributional-to-pointwise promotion.

Conditional reductions.

  13. Reduction to the WMH (Theorems 8.14, 7.19). The full Collatz conjecture reduces to the
       Weak Mixing Hypothesis ( K < 0.557) with a 4.65× safety margin. The WMH remains
       open.

  14. Fiber-57 reduction to Conjecture 12.2. The exact known-gap depth-2 partial kernel ( =
       129/1024) and the bottleneck inequality reduce the conjecture to an orbitwise anti-concentration
       bound on the q  3 return channel. (The value 129/1024 arises from the gap-2 and gap-5
       channels only; it does not include unresolved q  3 returns with gap  6, and is therefore
       not asserted as the full bottleneck constant of the dynamics.)

                                                             7
  15. Route B: Reduction to the CIC (Theorem 10.15, Section 10). The Carry Contamina-
       tion Theorem proves exact 1/4 elimination at every Sturmian depth. Combined with
       the Dichotomy (Theorem 10.9) and class-3 recurrence (Theorem 10.12), the full Collatz
       conjecture reduces to the CIC (Conjecture 10.14).

  16. Architectural Diagnosis (Remark 9.155). The ensemble side is exact and complete. The
       sole remaining gap in both routes is the distributional-to-pointwise barrier.

Reading guide

The paper has three layers, and readers may enter at different points depending on interest.

Layer 1: Conditional reduction (Sections 2­7,  45 pages; supplementary DAG analysis in
Appendix A). The [Core] results form a linear chain: Scrambling Lemma  Known-Zone
Decay  1/4 Law  Gap Distribution  Burst-Gap Criterion  Census Depth  Phantom
Universality  Per-Orbit Gain Rate  Robustness Corollary. Together with the Weak Mixing
Hypothesis (Hypothesis 8.3) as sole external input, this chain produces a conditional convergence
theorem. A referee evaluating the conditional reduction need read only this layer.

Layer 2: Quantitative attack on the open hypothesis (Section 9,  25 pages). Five indepen-
dent approaches to the WMH, culminating in an exact ensemble theory and an unconditional
almost-all crossing theorem: (i) Walsh­Fourier spectral analysis; (ii) odd-skeleton drift cross-
ing with ensemble CLT; (iii) modular crossing strata resolving 91% of odd starts at depth 13;
(iv) oscillation-factor unboundedness; (v) exact block law, i.i.d. cycle types, and Cramér-type
crossing bound. Tables 3, 4, and 5 catalogue all results with explicit status labels.

Layer 2b: Fiber-57 information bottleneck (Appendix B,  8 pages). An independent reduction
via the fiber-57 return structure. The pair-return automaton, bounded invariant core (|Ir| = 5),
absorption bottleneck lemma, and branch anti-concentration reduction together concentrate the
remaining obstruction into the inequality c < c0 = log2(1024/129)  2.989 on a 5-element
invariant core. This layer is self-contained and can be read independently of Layer 2.

Layer 2c: Sturmian obstruction, cross-core dynamics, and compound carry analysis (Section 10,
 30 pages). An independent reduction via the 2-adic Cantor set C. The Carry Contamina-
tion Theorem, the (3/4)D survivor count, refined mod-16/mod-32 transitions, and the class-3
bottleneck reduce Collatz to the Carry Independence Conjecture. The seven-block cross-core
alphabet extends the analysis to periodic elimination (through period 13), the 2-adic expander
property, unconditional measure shrinkage µ2(Tj)  0.522jD, the j  5 non-descending bound,
exhaustion-sequence rigidity, and the compound carry anti-correlation factor S2/S12  0.635.
This layer is self-contained and can be read independently of Layers 2 and 2b.

Layer 3: Exploratory and visualization (Appendix D and Section 12). Touch-growth geometry,
below-start visualizations, and the human­LLM collaboration methodology. These are not
load-bearing for any theorem.

Distributional vs. orbitwise statements. Throughout, modular and uniform-lift results
are distributional statements over residue classes ("on the ensemble"). Orbitwise consequences
require separate hypotheses or reductions and are labeled explicitly. When used informally,
"proved (ens.)" indicates ensemble-level results, while "proved" without qualification indicates
results that hold for every orbit.

Terminology. We use "persistent" and "safe" as shorthand for precise arithmetic conditions:
a step is persistent if v2(3n + 1) = 1, and safe if v2(3n + 1)  2.

                                                             8
Definition 1.1 (Single-hypothesis reduction). We say the Collatz conjecture admits a single-
hypothesis reduction when all remaining unresolved steps are concentrated into a single explicitly
stated orbitwise input, while the surrounding algebraic and combinatorial framework is proved.
This does not mean the remaining hypothesis is easy; the orbitwise input may be as difficult
as the original conjecture. The value of the reduction is organizational: it locates the difficulty
precisely. In the present work, the sole remaining input for the Route A conditional reduction
is the Weak Mixing Hypothesis (Hypothesis 8.3), equivalently the information-rate inequality
c < c0 of the fiber-57 programme. Other routes isolate parallel residual obstructions, notably
the CIC in Route B and D  Q for L > 116 in the cycle-exclusion programme.

Proof architecture and tier classification

Figure 1 displays the dependency structure of the conditional proof. All results are classified into
three tiers: [Core] results form the load-bearing proof chain; [Supporting] results provide
independent evidence but are not logically required; [Exploratory] results are visualization-
guided observations. A referee can verify the conditional reduction by reading only the [Core]
tier.

Proposition 1.2 (Core-spine sufficiency [Core]). The conditional reduction from the Orbit
Equidistribution Conjecture or the Weak Mixing Hypothesis to the Collatz conjecture depends
only on the Tier 1 core spine displayed in Figure 1. In particular, the carry-word, periodic-
core, and uniqueness-threshold sections (Sections 7.7­7.13) are logically independent supporting
material and are not required for the proof of the conditional reduction theorem.

Proof. We trace dependencies only.
    Step 1 (burst­gap route). The conditional convergence theorem (Theorem 8.1) combines

the entry­occupancy/descent argument with the Burst­Gap Criterion (Theorem 4.9). The
latter requires the modular distributional inputs supplied by the 1/4 Persistent-Transition Law
(Theorem 3.1) and the Modular Gap Distribution (Lemma 4.4). These in turn are founded
on the Scrambling Lemma (Theorem 5.1) and Known-Zone Decay (Theorem 6.1). Thus the
burst­gap route depends only on the first five nodes in the core spine.

    Step 2 (phantom route). The phantom route uses Universal Census Depth (Proposition 7.11)
to remove the census constant from the gain formula, Phantom Universality (Theorem 7.15) to
identify the relevant family class, and the Per-Orbit Gain Rate (Theorem 7.19) to show that
the total amortised phantom gain stays below the drift budget . The Robustness Corollary
(Corollary 7.23) then shows that exact orbitwise uniformity is not necessary: any summable
depthwise mixing error below the stated threshold suffices. This gives the final conditional
reduction under either the Orbit Equidistribution Conjecture or the Weak Mixing Hypothesis
(Theorem 8.16).

    Step 3 (non-dependence of later sections). The carry-word autocorrelation, periodic-core,
defect-tail, and uniqueness-threshold results are not invoked in the proofs of the Per-Orbit Gain
Rate theorem, the Robustness Corollary, or the final conditional reduction. They therefore pro-
vide additional structure and possible future routes, but are not load-bearing for the conditional
theorem.

    Combining Steps 1­3 proves the proposition.

Conditional reductions. The following results are proved modulo an additional orbitwise
truncation/tail condition:

   · Theorem 8.14, which reduces convergence to the agreement between orbit averages and
       ensemble expectations for truncated observables.

   · Theorem 8.16, which reduces WMH to convergence through the bounded-gain regime,
       subject to the same truncation upgrade.

Open step (orbitwise upgrade). The remaining gap is the passage from fixed-depth,
bounded observables to full, unbounded orbit statistics. Concretely, one must establish an
orbitwise tail-vanishing condition of the form

 lim lim sup                     1   N      - K)+  =  0
                                 N
K N                                    (Fi

                                    i=1

for the burst and gap observables. This step is equivalent in spirit to upgrading ensemble-level
laws to pointwise control along a single trajectory, and is not resolved in the present work.

Interpretation. Accordingly, this paper should be viewed as a structural and quantitative
reduction of the Collatz conjecture to an explicit orbitwise condition, together with several
independent exact and asymptotic results. It is not a complete proof of the conjecture.

    In particular, the main obstruction is isolated as a single orbitwise regularity condition
(Weak Mixing / tail control), rather than distributed across multiple interacting components.

2 Notation and preliminaries

We collect the formal definitions and basic facts used throughout the paper.

Definition 2.1 (Standard Collatz map). The standard Collatz map C : N  N is C(n) = n/2
if n is even, and C(n) = 3n + 1 if n is odd.

Conjecture 2.2 (Collatz conjecture). For every N0  1, the orbit N0, C(N0), C2(N0), . . .
eventually reaches 1.

Definition 2.3 (Syracuse map). For an odd integer n  1, define the Syracuse map T (n) :=
(3n + 1)/2v2(3n+1), where v2(m) := max{j : 2j | m} is the 2-adic valuation. Write n = 2km - 1
with m odd, k = v2(n + 1)  1. We call k the odd-run length and m the multiplier. Here m is
the odd multiplier associated with the odd integer n.

Definition 2.4 (Persistent and safe states). A state (k, µ) where µ = m mod 8 and k  2
is persistent if 3kµ  7 (mod 8). A state with k = 1 (or a persistent state whose certified
worst-case drift w¯ = k log2 3 - (k + emin) < 0) is safe. The persistent class P is the set of odd
integers n such that (k, m mod 8) is persistent.

Definition 2.5 (Burst-gap decomposition). Given a Syracuse orbit x0, x1, x2, . . ., a burst is a
maximal run of consecutive epochs with kt  2 (states with odd-run length at least 2). A gap is
a maximal run with kt = 1 (downward or safe iterates). The orbit decomposes as an alternating
sequence:

                                                L1 G1 L2 G2 · · ·

                                                                 burst gap burst gap

where Li is the length of the i-th burst and Gi the length of the i-th gap.

                                                             11
    gap: downward (k = 1)      Persistent Exit Lemma

L1    G1                   L2            G2         L3         G3            L4

3 24  11  2 325                      1      1      23          11  1  2 4 32     time t

     burst: k  2

Figure 2: The burst-gap decomposition of a Collatz orbit. Bursts (red) are maximal runs of
iterates with k  2; gaps (green) are runs of downward iterates (k = 1). The Persistent Exit
Lemma (Lemma 4.4) shows that when a burst ends at a persistent state, the subsequent gap
has length exactly 1.

Definition 2.6 (Odd-to-odd map). Define

                                     T (n)  =      3n + 1
                                                  2v2(3n+1) ,

which maps an odd integer to the next odd integer in the Collatz sequence.

Definition 2.7 (Gap map). Let g denote the length of a gap, i.e. a maximal run of odd-to-odd
steps with k = 1 (equivalently, states outside the burst regime k  2; see Definition 2.5). The
corresponding gap map is the g-fold iterate

                                                            T g,

applied over these g successive odd-to-odd steps.
    For n in a fixed residue class a mod 2M,

                                     T g(n)    =  3gn +  cg ,
                                                     2V

where

                                                                                       g

                                                       V = vi

                                                                                     i=1

is the total number of factors of 2 removed along those g steps, and cg is the correction term
determined by the halving pattern.

Interpretation. The map T represents a single odd-to-odd step in the Collatz sequence. Its
iterate T g denotes g successive odd-to-odd steps.

    When g is the length of a gap (i.e., a maximal run of steps with v2(3n + 1)  2), the iterate
T g corresponds to evolution across that gap phase, and is referred to as the gap map.

Definition 2.8 (Critical persistent frequency). Define

                               crit  :=     wS-      0.539,
                                         wP+ + wS-

where wP+ = supxP w¯(x) is the worst-case certified weight from a persistent state, and wS- =
infx/P (-w¯(x)) > 0 is the best guaranteed descent from a safe state. If lim sup NP (T )/T < crit

for an orbit, then the orbit's long-run mean drift is negative, forcing convergence.

We also record a simple but essential fact about modular arithmetic:

Lemma 2.9 (Bijection lemma). Let a be an odd integer and K  1. Then the map  
a ·  mod 2K is a bijection on {0, 1, . . . , 2K - 1}.

Proof. Since gcd(a, 2K) = 1 (as a is odd), multiplication by a is an automorphism of Z/2KZ.

                                               12
3 The 1/4 Persistent-Transition Law

This section proves that the uniform-lift persistent-to-persistent transition probability is exactly
1/4. This is a structural fact about the arithmetic of the 3n + 1 map at the modular level.

Theorem 3.1 (1/4 Persistent-Transition Law [Core]). For every persistent state (k, µ mod 8)

with k  2, the fraction of admissible lifts m  µ (mod 8) whose successor under the odd-to-odd

map  T  is  again  persistent  equals  exactly    1  .  That  is,
                                                  4

                   lim  #{m            N  :  m    µ     (8),  k    2,  3k m       7   (8)}     =  1
                                                                                                   .
                   N                      #{m  N : m  µ (8)}                                      4

Proof. We compute directly. Write P = 3km. Since the state is persistent, P  7 (mod 8), so
P - 1  6 (mod 8) and e := v2(P - 1) = 1. The successor odd integer is n = (P - 1)/2, which
satisfies n  3 (mod 4). The successor's odd-run length is k = v2(n + 1) = v2((P + 1)/2).

    Since P  7 (mod 8), we have P + 1  0 (mod 8), so (P + 1)/2  0 (mod 4), giving k  2.
Precisely, k = j  2 iff v2(P + 1) = j + 1, i.e. P  2j+1 - 1 (mod 2j+2).

    The successor multiplier is m = (P + 1)/2j+1, which is odd. The successor state (j, m) is
persistent iff 3jm  7 (mod 8). Since 3j mod 8 cycles as 3, 1, 3, 1, . . . for j = 1, 2, 3, 4, . . ., this

reduces to:
                                         m  7 (mod 8) if j is even,
                                                  5 (mod 8) if j is odd.

In either case, exactly one of the four residue classes {1, 3, 5, 7} (mod 8) satisfies the condition.

As P ranges over integers  2j+1 - 1 (mod 2j+2), the four lifts to mod 2j+4 produce m =

(P + 1)/2j+1  {1, 3, 5, 7} (mod 8), which is exactly uniformly distributed.

Therefore, for each fixed j  2: Pr[successor persistent | k = j] = 1/4. Since this holds for

every j,

                        Pr[successor persistent] =                 1   · Pr[k  =  j]  =  1  .
                                                                   4                     4

                                                              j2

Corollary 3.2 (Geometric persistent excursions). In the uniform-lift model, persistent run
lengths follow a Geometric(3/4) distribution with mean 4/3.

Proof. Theorem 3.1 applies uniformly to every persistent state (k, µ) with k  2: the continu-
ation probability is 1/4 regardless of the specific values of k and µ. Since this holds at every
persistent state, the continuation probability is memoryless along the persistent run, and suc-
cessive applications give Pr[run  L] = (1/4)L-1. The run length is therefore Geometric(3/4)
with mean 1/(3/4) = 4/3.

Remark 3.3 (Expected burst length). A burst is a maximal run of consecutive odd-to-odd
epochs with kt  2 (Definition 2.5). By Lemma 4.4 (Part 1), the valuation kt decreases by
exactly 1 at each step within a burst, so a burst entered at k = k0 lasts exactly k0 - 1 epochs
(terminating when kt reaches 2 and the next step gives kt+1 = 1).

    Under equidistribution, the entry valuation k0 has distribution Pr(k0 = j) = 2-(j-1) for
j  2 (Lemma 4.7), giving expected burst length E[k0 - 1] = E[k0] - 1 = 3 - 1 = 2 epochs.

    This is the value used in Corollary 4.8.

Remark 3.4 (Significance). The 1/4 law is an exact structural result, not a heuristic approxi-
mation. It shows that at the modular level, the 3n + 1 map has a built-in exit mechanism from
persistent states: exactly 3/4 of lifts escape to a safe state at each step. The conjecture reduces
to showing that actual orbits cannot systematically concentrate on the persistent class beyond
the crit threshold.

                                                        13
4 The convergence chain

This section develops a conditional convergence chain linking the Collatz conjecture to two
statistical hypotheses on orbit structure: a mean burst bound and a mean gap bound. The
individual links in the chain (Entry­Occupancy Equivalence, Entry Bound, and the Burst-Gap
Criterion) are each proved unconditionally, but the hypotheses they require remain open.

4.1 Entry­Occupancy Equivalence

Theorem 4.1 (Entry­Occupancy Equivalence). Let x0, x1, x2, . . . be the Syracuse orbit, and
let P denote the persistent class. Define

             NP (T ) := #{0  t < T : xt  P }, EP (T ) := #{0  t < T : xt+1  P }.

Then lim supT  EP (T )/T = lim supT  NP (T )/T .
Proof. Relabelling s = t + 1: EP (T ) = #{1  s  T : xs  P }, while NP (T +1) = #{0  s 
T : xs  P }. These differ by at most 1P (x0), so

                                     |EP (T )/T - NP (T +1)/T |  1/T  0.

Since NP (T + 1)/(T + 1) · (T + 1)/T  NP (T + 1)/(T + 1) and (T + 1)/T  1, the lim sup
values coincide.

Remark 4.2. This equivalence is elementary but essential: it allows us to reason about persis-
tent occupancy (a static count) via persistent entries (a dynamic transition count), the latter
being more directly related to the burst-gap structure.

4.2 Entry bound implies convergence

Theorem 4.3 (Entry bound implies convergence). If there exists p < crit such that every
orbit satisfies lim supT  EP (T )/T  p, then every orbit of the Collatz map converges.

Proof. By Theorem 4.1, lim sup NP (T )/T  p < crit. For every orbit prefix of length T , the
cumulative certified drift satisfies

1  T -1           NP (T    )  wP+    -  1 - NP (T )  wS-.
T                    T                          T
        w¯(xt)

   t=0

This is because each persistent epoch contributes at most wP+ to the drift, while each safe epoch
contributes at most -wS- < 0.

    Taking lim sup and using lim sup NP (T )/T  p < crit = wS-/(wP+ + wS-):

lim sup  1  T -1           pwP+ - (1 - p)wS-         <  0.
         T
 T               w¯(xt)

            t=0

The long-run mean certified drift is strictly negative. By the standard descent argument (see,
e.g., [17, 10]): a negative long-run mean certified drift implies that the orbit must eventually
descend below its starting value. By strong induction (every value below the start has already
been shown to converge), the orbit converges to the trivial cycle {1, 4, 2}.

                           14
4.3 The Persistent Exit Lemma

Lemma 4.4 (Persistent Exit Lemma [Core]). Let x0, x1, . . . be a Syracuse orbit with burst-gap
decomposition.

1. Every burst terminates at a state with kt = 2.

2. When the final burst state is persistent (i.e., 32µ  7 (mod 8), equivalently mt  7
   (mod 8)), the subsequent gap has length exactly 1: the first gap iterate's Syracuse successor
   re-enters a burst immediately.

3. Under the uniform-lift model, the 1/4 Persistent-Transition Law (Theorem 3.1) gives

     Pr[successor  is   persistent]  =  1   at  each   persistent   state.
                                        4

Proof. Part (1): Bursts terminate at k = 2. If kt  3, then xt  7 (mod 8) (since
xt = 2ktmt - 1 with 2ktmt  0 (mod 8) for kt  3), and

                                    T (xt)  =   3xt +  1  =   3  ·  2kt-1mt  -  1,
                                                   2

since v2(3xt + 1) = v2(3 · 2ktmt - 2) = 1 for kt  2. The successor has kt+1 = kt - 1  2, so the
burst continues. Therefore the burst can only end when kt = 2.

Part (2): Persistent final state  gap length 1. Let xt be the final state of a burst with
kt = 2 and mt  7 (mod 8) (persistent). Write xt = 4mt - 1.

    The first gap iterate. 3xt + 1 = 12mt - 2 = 2(6mt - 1). Since mt is odd, 6mt - 1 is odd, so
v2(3xt + 1) = 1. Thus xt+1 = 6mt - 1 with kt+1 = 1 (safe).

    The second iterate. 3xt+1 + 1 = 18mt - 2 = 2(9mt - 1). Since mt  7 (mod 8), we have
9mt - 1  62  6 (mod 8), giving v2(9mt - 1) = 1. Therefore v2(3xt+1 + 1) = 2, so

                                     xt+2   =   18mt      -  2  =   9mt -  1
                                                     4                  2   .

Now  k(xt+2) = v2(xt+2 + 1) = v2        9mt+1   .  Writing       mt = 7 + 8r, we get 9mt + 1 = 64 + 72r =
                                            2
                                                                9mt+1
8(8 + 9r),  so  v2(9mt  + 1)    3,  hence   k(xt+2)    =  v2        2   2.

Since k(xt+2)  2, the state xt+2 begins a new burst. The gap consists of the single

iterate xt+1, so Gi = 1.

Part (3) is a restatement of Theorem 3.1.

Remark 4.5 (Gaps of length 2 do occur). An earlier version of this paper claimed that no gap
has length exactly 2. This claim is false: computational verification shows that gaps of length 2
constitute approximately 19% of all gaps across typical orbits. The smallest counterexample is
n0 = 3: the orbit 3  5  1  · · · has burst {3} (k = 2, m = 1, non-persistent), followed
by gap {5, 1} of length 2. Another example is n0 = 71: the burst {71, 107} ends at the non-
persistent state 107 (k = 2, m = 27, 27 mod 8 = 3), followed by gap {161, 121} of length 2.

    The error arose from assuming that every burst ends at a persistent state. In fact, bursts
whose final state has kt = 2 but mt  7 (mod 8) are non-persistent, and the gap structure
following such states is unconstrained. The Persistent Exit Lemma correctly identifies the
subcase where Gi = 1 is guaranteed.

Lemma 4.6 (Modular gap distribution [Core]). Let n be an odd integer in a gap step (v2(3n +
1) = 1, i.e. n  3 (mod 4)). Over the uniform distribution on integers n  3 (mod 4) mod-
ulo 22+L (for any L  1), the gap length G satisfies

                   Pr(G = g) = 2-g for 1  g  L, Pr(G > L) = 2-L.

Equivalently, the memoryless        property Pr(G  g + 1 | G  g) =                  1  holds for every g  1.  In
                                                                                    2

particular, G  Geometric(1/2) with E[G] = 2.

                                                          15
Proof. We show that each successive step of the gap is decided by exactly one bit of n, with
the two outcomes equally likely.
Step 1 (bit at position 2). For n  3 (mod 4), the Syracuse step gives T (n) = (3n + 1)/2.
Split on n mod 8:

   · n  3 (mod 8): T (n) = (24k + 10)/2 = 12k + 5  1 (mod 4), so v2(3 T (n) + 1)  2. The
       gap ends (burst starts).

   · n  7 (mod 8): T (n) = (24k + 22)/2 = 12k + 11  3 (mod 4), so v2(3 T (n) + 1) = 1. The
       gap continues.

Among integers  3 (mod 4), exactly half fall in each case, so Pr(G  2) = 1/2.
Inductive step (bit at position 2 + g). For the continuing case (n  7 mod 8), T (n)  3
(mod 4) is again a gap step. Whether the gap continues at step g + 1 depends on T (g)(n) mod 8,
which is determined by one additional bit of n at the next modular depth. Since the gap-step
map n  (3n + 1)/2 is injective (with inverse n = (2m - 1)/3), the two sub-classes at each
depth have equal size.

    By induction, Pr(G  g + 1 | G  g) = 1/2 for all g  1, giving the geometric distribution.

Lemma 4.7 (Modular valuation distribution). For odd n in a burst step (n  1 (mod 4)), the
2-adic valuation k = v2(3n + 1) satisfies, over uniform lifts at each successive modular depth,

                                         Pr(k = j) = 2-(j-1) for j  2.

In particular, E[k | k  2] = 3.

Proof. For n  1 (mod 4), we have 3n + 1  4 (mod 8). Split on the next bit:

   · n  1 (mod 8): 3n + 1 = 4(6j + 1) with 6j + 1 odd, so v2 = 2.

   · n  5 (mod 8): 3n + 1 = 8(3j + 2), so v2  3.

Half the lifts give k = 2; the other half give k  3. For the k  3 case, the same splitting applies
at the next depth: half give k = 3, half give k  4, and so on. By induction, Pr(k = j) = 2-(j-1)
for j  2, giving E[k] = j2 j · 2-(j-1) = 3.

Corollary 4.8 (Convergence prediction under equidistribution). Under the equidistributed mod-
ular model, the expected log-contraction per burst-gap cycle is strictly negative:

        E[B]  log 3 - E[k] log 2          +  E[G]  log  3  =  2   (log  3  -  3  log  2)  +  2  log  3      -1.15  <   0,
                                                        2                                            2

where E[B] = 2 (Corollary 3.2), E[G] = 2 (Lemma 4.6), and E[k | k  2] = 3 (Lemma 4.7).
    Thus, even though gaps can have arbitrary length (Remark 4.5), the deeper contraction

during burst steps (E[k] = 3 rather than the minimum k = 2) more than compensates for
the longer gaps. The false Gap Lemma (Gi = 1 always) was unnecessary: the geometric gap
distribution E[G] = 2 suffices for convergence under equidistribution.

Remark 4.10 (Role of the two hypotheses). Both Hypothesis A and Hypothesis B are open
orbitwise conjectures. Under equidistribution, the expected burst length is 2 (Corollary 3.2) and
the expected gap length is 2 (Lemma 4.6). Both hypotheses follow from the Orbit Equidistri-
bution Conjecture (Theorem 8.14): Hypothesis B via the coupling inequality at fixed modulus
(Step 2), and Hypothesis A via the growing-moduli tail control (Step 2).

    The Persistent Exit Lemma provides structural support: it shows that when a burst ends at
a persistent state, the subsequent gap has length exactly 1. More generally, the Modular Gap
Distribution Lemma (Lemma 4.6) proves that gap length is Geometric(1/2) with E[G] = 2, and
Corollary 4.8 shows that this suffices for convergence under equidistribution.

5 The Scrambling Lemma

This is the algebraic core of the paper. We show that the gap map introduces zero carries
between the known and unknown parts of an integer, yielding an exact bijection on high bits.

5.1 Statement and proof

Theorem 5.1 (Scrambling Lemma [Core]). Let n  a (mod 2M) with n  7 (mod 16), where
M  is chosen so that the halving pattern (v1, . . . , vg) of the odd-to-odd steps is constant on the
class a mod 2M. Write n = a +  · 2M with   0.

    Then the odd-to-odd step satisfies

                                     T (g)(n)     =  3ga + cg  + 3g  ·  · 2M-V ,                                      (1)
                                                        2V

where g, V =   g      vi, and cg depend           only on a   (not on ).                                       2K-M 
               i=1    = 1, the map                3g ·  mod   2K-M is a
   Since gcd(3g, 2)
                                                                           bijection  on  {0,  1,  .  .  .  ,         -  1}

(Lemma 2.9).

   Therefore:

                                                          17
1. The bits of T (g)(n) at positions  M  - V are an exact bijection of the free parameter .

2. If  is uniformly distributed on {0, 1, . . . , 2K-M - 1}, then each bit of T (g)(n) at position
   j  M  - V is an exactly unbiased coin flip, independently of a.

Proof. The odd-to-odd step computes T (g)(n) = (3gn + cg)/2V , where the correction cg arises
from the iterated 3n + 1 steps. Precisely, if we write the g-step iteration as

T (g)(n) = 3gn +                           g-1  3g-1-i  ·  2si
                                           i=0

                                           2V

for certain shift terms si determined by the halving pattern, then cg =  g-1  3g-1-i · 2si  depends
only on the halving pattern, hence only on a mod 2M.                     i=0

    Now substitute n = a +  · 2M:

3gn + cg = 3g(a +  · 2M) + cg

                              = (3ga + cg) + 3g ·  · 2M.                                    (2)

This is the key algebraic step.

Affine dependence on . The dependence on  is exactly affine: the term 3g ·  · 2M is a pure
multiple of 2M, so the decomposition (2) is an exact identity of integers, not an approximation.
No properties of carry propagation are needed; the identity follows from the distributivity of
multiplication over addition.

Division by 2V . Dividing (2) by 2V :

T (g)(n)                      =  3ga + cg  + 3g  ·  · 2M-V .
                                    2V

The first term is a fixed integer (since 2V | 3ga + cg by the halving pattern), independent of .
The second term contributes to bits at positions  M  - V through the map   3g · . Since
3g is odd, this is a bijection on Z/2K-MZ by Lemma 2.9.

    Hence modulo any power of two above the threshold M  - V , the map on  is a bijective

affine transformation. This is the only property needed for the scrambling effect.

Remark 5.2 (Why this is not obvious). The naive concern is that the iterated 3n + 1 compu-
tation produces carries that propagate from low bits to high bits, destroying any independence.
The Scrambling Lemma shows this fear is unfounded: the dependence on the unknown parame-
ter  is exactly affine, entering as 3g · 2M-V after division. Since 3g is odd, this is an invertible
linear map modulo any power of two. The "scrambling" is therefore a consequence of linearity
and coprimality, not of any carry-cancellation mechanism.

5.2 The pattern-determination bound

The following bound controls how many bits are needed to determine the halving pattern:

Proposition 5.3 (Pattern-determination bound [Supporting]). The modulus M  required to
fix the halving pattern satisfies

                              M  - M  max(0, g - 2),                                        (3)

and, combined with V  g + 1:

                              M  - V  M - 3.                                                (4)

This bound is independent of the gap length g.

                                 18
Proof. We establish (3) using two facts:

1. For the gap-entry residue classes considered here (n  7 (mod 16)), the first two halvings
   satisfy v1 = v2 = 1. Indeed, the entry condition n  7 (mod 16) forces 3n + 1  22
   (mod 48), giving v1 = 1. The next iterate (3n + 1)/2 satisfies (3n + 1)/2  11 (mod 24),
   and 3 · 11 + 1 = 34 = 2 · 17, confirming v2 = 1. (The computation of v1 and v2 requires
   only the bits of n up to position 3, which are determined by n mod 16 = 7; this does not
   hold for arbitrary odd n.)

2. Each subsequent halving vi (i  3) depends on at most one additional bit of n beyond
   those already consumed by the first i - 1 halvings. This gives M   M + (g - 2) additional
   bits for g  3, and M  = M for g  2.

For the total halvings: V =  g    vi       g + 1.1  Therefore,  in  the  generic  case  (V   g + 1):
                             i=1

M  - V  M + (g - 2) - (g + 1) = M - 3.

In the pure-gap case (V = g, all vi = 1):
                                      M  - V  M + (g - 2) - g = M - 2.

Both bounds are independent of g.

Remark 5.4. The bound M  -V  M -2 (worst case) means that each odd-to-odd step reduces
the known zone by at least 2 bits, regardless of the gap length. When at least one burst step
occurs (vj  2 for some j), the stronger bound M  - V  M - 3 holds, giving 3-bit decay per
step. This is the quantitative content of the "scrambling": longer gaps do not help the known
zone grow, because the additional bits needed to determine the halving pattern are compensated
by the additional halvings.

    We now summarize the relationships among the components established above. Figure 3
shows which results are proved unconditionally and which inputs remain open conjectures.

    As shown in Figure 3, the burst­gap conditional framework reduces the Collatz conjecture to
the Orbit Equidistribution Conjecture via two intermediate orbitwise hypotheses (mean burst
and mean gap bounds), both of which are derived from equidistribution in Theorem 8.14.
The phantom-cycle analysis developed in Sections 7­7.6 (Figure 5) provides a complementary
perspective: it quantifies the expanding-family obstacle directly and shows it is controlled with
a large safety margin.

6 Known-Zone Decay

We iterate the Scrambling Lemma to show that the known zone shrinks to zero in M/2 steps.

Theorem 6.1 (Known-Zone Decay [Core]). Starting from a class a mod 2M with M  4, let
T k(n) denote k iterated odd-to-odd steps. Define the known zone Zk as the number of low-order
bits of T k(n) that are determined by the starting class a. Then:

   1. Z0 = M .

   2. Zk+1  max(0, Zk - 2) for each k.

    1In a pure gap traversal with vi = 1 for all i, V = g exactly. The bound V  g + 1 holds when the sequence of
odd-to-odd steps includes at least one burst step (vj  2 for some j), which is the generic case in the Known-Zone
Decay iteration since the gap terminates and the subsequent burst step contributes v  2. When the Scrambling
Lemma is applied to a pure gap of length g (all vi = 1), the correct bound is V = g, giving M  - V  M - 2.

Figure 3: Architecture of the burst­gap conditional framework (Sections 4­6). Green boxes

3. After M/2 odd-to-odd steps, Zk = 0: no low-order bits of T k(n) remain determined by
   the starting class. Moreover, the output bits are an exact affine-bijective function of the
   free parameters introduced during the iteration; hence they are exactly uniform whenever
   those free parameters are uniformly distributed.

Proof. We proceed by induction on k.

Base case: Z0 = M by definition.

Inductive step: Suppose at step k, the iterate T k(n) is known modulo 2Zk (i.e. the low Zk
bits are determined by the starting class a).

    Apply the Scrambling Lemma (Theorem 5.1) with the known modulus M = Zk: the odd-
to-odd step produces T k+1(n) with known zone Zk+1 = Mk - Vk, where:

   · Mk  Zk + (gk - 2) by Proposition 5.3, where gk is the k-th gap length.

· Vk  gk (total halvings in the k-th odd-to-odd step, with equality in the pure-gap case
   vi = 1 for all i; see the footnote in Proposition 5.3).

Therefore:

               Zk+1 = Mk - Vk  Zk + (gk - 2) - gk = Zk - 2.

Since Zk decreases by at least 2 per step and Zk  0 by definition:

                              Zk+1  max(0, Zk - 2).

Termination: Starting from Z0 = M , after k = M/2 steps:

                                               Zk  M - 2M/2  0.

At this point, Zk = 0: no bits of T k(n) are determined by the starting class. The bits at
positions  0 are an exact bijection of the free parameters accumulated through k odd-to-odd
steps, and if those parameters are uniformly distributed, so are the output bits.

(all vi = 1). Empirically, in the tested residue classes (M  18), the average decay exceeds
2.8 bits per step, and the known zone reaches 0 in 1­3 steps for typical starting classes (see

Remark 6.3 (Computational verification). For M = 12: the worst-case bound gives Zk = 0
after 12/2 = 6 odd-to-odd steps. Across all 239 testable residue classes a mod 212 with a  7
(mod 16) (the remaining 17 classes reach 1 before an odd-to-odd step), the minimum observed
shrinkage V - (M  - M ) is 3 (exceeding the worst-case bound of 2), confirming (4). Empirically,
the known zone reaches 0 in 1­3 odd-to-odd steps for all tested classes (at M = 12).

Remark 6.4 (Relation to mixing properties). The Known-Zone Decay is an exact information-
loss statement: the number of low-order bits determined by the initial residue class shrinks by
at least 2 per odd-to-odd step, with no error term. This exactness is local and algebraic; it
does not by itself imply orbitwise mixing or equidistribution. The transfer from information
loss to distributional uniformity requires that the free parameters introduced at each step are
uniformly distributed (the uniform-lift assumption).

7 Phantom cycles and the affine census theorem

The preceding sections analyzed Collatz dynamics through burst-gap structure, scrambling, and
modular ranking. We now develop a complementary line of attack: a deterministic census of how
orbits interact with phantom cycles, modular attractors of the accelerated Syracuse map whose
2-adic roots repel real orbits. The main result of this section is the Affine Census Theorem
(Theorem 7.9), which provides a uniform bound on the number of orbit points that can shadow
any given phantom family.

                               21
7.1 Phantom cycles and 2-adic roots

Definition 7.1 (Signature and block map). A signature is a tuple  = (k1, . . . , k) of positive
integers. The associated block map F : Z2  Z2 is defined by applying  successive Syracuse
steps with prescribed 2-adic valuations:

F(x) = T ()(x), where v2(3xj + 1) = kj+1 for j = 0, . . . ,  - 1,

Remark 7.3 (Why "phantom"). The root  is a formal 2-adic integer, not a positive integer:
if 3 < 2K then  > 0 but generically  is enormous, while if 3 > 2K then  < 0. In
neither case does  lie in the range of a convergent Collatz orbit. However,  mod 2K defines
a legitimate residue class, and real integers in this class undergo the block map F with the
prescribed valuation pattern. Thus phantom cycles create modular "shadows" that real orbits
can temporarily follow.

Proposition 7.4 (2-adic repulsion [Supporting]). For any x   (mod 2m) with m  K,

                   v2 F(x) -  = v2(x - ) - K.

That is, each application of the block map decreases the 2-adic alignment with  by exactly K
bits.

Proof. Write x =  + 2mu with u odd. Then F(x) = F() + (3/2K ) · 2mu =  + 3 · 2m-K · u.
Since 3 and u are both odd, v2(F(x) - ) = m - K.

holds.

Proof. Write D = A - , so we count solutions to v2(D + Bu)  a, i.e. D + Bu  0 (mod 2a).

7.5 Gamma monotonicity and the uniform bound

Proposition 7.8 (Gamma monotonicity). Starting from the exit class e with B0 = 2K (so
0 = K), the affine iteration produces a sequence 0 > 1 > 2 > · · · that is strictly decreasing.
The iteration terminates (affine structure dissolves) after at most K steps.

Proof. In the pure case, s+1 = s - s with s  1, so  decreases by at least 1. In the equal
and reverse cases, the sub-populations have  < s (by splitting on u). Since s is a positive

integer that strictly decreases, it reaches 0 in at most K steps.

Theorem 7.9 (Uniform multistep census bound). For each phantom family (, ) with exit
class e and depth K, there exists a finite constant

                                   Ce := max 2min(s,s),                                   (8)
                                     0sK

where (s, s) are the parameters of the affine iteration starting from (A0, B0) = (e, 2K), such
that for all s  1, L  1, and a  1:

N (s,  a, L) := #{u  [0, 2L) : v2 T s(e + 2K u) -   a}  Ce · 2L-a.

Proof. Combine Lemmas 7.6­7.7 with Proposition 7.8.
    At each step s of the affine iteration, the census ratio is 2min(s,s) by Lemma 7.7. Since s

strictly decreases (Proposition 7.8), the sequence min(s, s) is eventually zero.
    When the affine iteration encounters an equal-case split (Lemma 7.6(b)), the population

divides into two sub-progressions of size 2L-1 each. Applying Lemma 7.7 to each sub-progression

and summing:

Ntotal( a)    Ceven             ·  2(L-1)-a  + Codd  · 2(L-1)-a  =  Ceven +Codd  · 2L-a.
                                                                           2

Since  decreases in both sub-progressions, Ceven, Codd  Cpre-split, so the effective census con-
stant does not increase through a split.

    Taking the maximum over all steps gives the uniform bound Ce · 2L-a.

Remark 7.10 (Computational verification). The census constants Ce = 2maxs min(s,K-Vs) in
Table 1 have been verified by brute-force enumeration for all families with K  12 (ell5, ell6,

ell7, ell8) at fidelity L = 14 and steps s = 1, . . . , . In every case, the observed census ratio
N (s,  a, L)/2L-a matches the predicted 2min(s,s) exactly, and the maximum over all s equals
2maxs min(s,K-Vs). The higher-K families (m10, m11, m20) are verified symbolically through

the carry-word formula.

                                             24
7.6 Universal census depth and the refined gain bound

The census constant Ce = 2maxs min(s,K-Vs) can be exponentially large in K (for instance,
Ce = 128 for ell8 with K = 10). However, the following proposition shows that the census
excess at intermediate steps does not represent genuine shadow gain.

Proposition 7.11 (Universal census depth [Core]). At step s of the affine iteration, assume
s < s (equivalently, s < K - Vs). Then every lift x = e + 2Ku satisfies

               v2 T s(x) -  = s (exactly).

The census excess 2s is therefore a uniform shift of the entire population, not a selective shadow
of specific orbits.

Proof. Write v2(T s(x) - ) = min v2(T s(x) - s), s , where s = Fs() (Proposition 7.26).
In the pure case of the affine iteration, v2 T s(x) - s = v2(x - ) - Vs. Since v2(x - )  K
for any lift in the exit class, v2 T s(x) - s  K - Vs = s > s. The minimum is therefore s
for every u, proving the claim.

Remark 7.12 (Computational verification). Proposition 7.11 has been verified by brute-force
enumeration at fidelity L = 10 (1024 lifts) for all seven known families at every step where
s < s. In every such case, all lifts produce v2 = s exactly. At steps where s  s (which
occurs only for ell6 at s  3), the valuations vary across lifts and the census ratio is 2s, reflecting
genuine orbit-tracking at the natural affine scale.

    Proposition 7.11 has a decisive consequence for the gain formula: the large census constant
Ce reflects a systematic proximity of s to  (the intrinsic near-return), not selective orbit
tracking. This proximity cancels over the full cycle, since after  steps the block map satisfies

F (x)  -    =  3   (x  -  ),  v2 F(x) -  = v2(x - ) - K.       (9)
               2K

Hence the end-of-cycle census ratio is 1: no excess persists.

Proposition 7.13 (Refined gain formula). Define the gain contribution of a phantom family 
as G() := /2K, where  =  log2 3 - K is the log-drift per block. Since the universal census
depth theorem (Proposition 7.11) shows that the end-of-cycle census ratio is 1, the gain per
family is /2K (the census constant Ce does not enter).

Proof. Equation (9) shows that the end-of-cycle census ratio is 1 for every phantom family. The
gain per family is therefore /2K, not  · Ce/2K.

Remark 7.14 (Global gain exceeds ). The quantity  = 2 - log2 3 is the average per-step
log-contraction of the Syracuse map. Summing /2K over all known phantom families at small
depth gives Gtotal  0.056; however, every primitive cyclic composition is a phantom family
(Section 7.6 below), so the global sum over all depths diverges beyond  (see Equation (10)).
The correct quantity to bound is the per-orbit gain rate (Theorem 7.19).

Figure 5: The phantom-cycle analysis. The four green boxes are proved unconditionally in

proof of convergence remains Theorem 8.14, which derives the needed burst­gap hypotheses
from equidistribution.

    The development of the phantom-cycle route involved several key pivots, each prompted by
a failed conjecture:
1. Census-constant bug (Session 10). A computational error in the Ce table was discovered

   and corrected. The fix revealed that Ce can grow exponentially in K for pathological sig-
   natures (m = O(K)), but the Universal Census Depth Theorem (Proposition 7.11) showed
   that Ce cancels from the gain formula entirely: the census excess is an intrinsic near-return,
   not selective orbit tracking.
2. Family-count conjecture fails. The natural next step: bounding the number of phantom
   families by a polynomial Kc: turned out to be false: the Phantom Universality Theorem
   (Theorem 7.15) shows that every cyclic composition is a phantom family, giving exponential
   growth  1.87K.
3. Global gain exceeds . With exponentially many families, the global gain sum /2K 
   0.587 exceeds the contraction budget   0.415, so the naïve proof strategy fails.
4. Per-orbit amortisation resolves the problem. The resolution: the budget applies per
   orbit, not globally. Amortising each family's gain over its shadow duration  gives the per-
   orbit rate R  0.088   (Theorem 7.19).

                                                26
The phantom-cycle analysis (Figure 5) provides independent, quantitative evidence that the Or-
bit Equidistribution Conjecture is the sole conceptual bottleneck: the expanding-family gain is
controlled with a 4.65× safety margin, and Corollary 7.23 shows that only summable TV decay:
far weaker than exact uniformity, is needed. The formal conditional reduction to equidistribu-
tion remains Theorem 8.14, which derives the burst­gap hypotheses as consequences.

The per-orbit gain rate.

The global gain  ()/2K() sums over all phantom families  with  > 0. By Theorem 7.15,
every primitive cyclic composition is a phantom family, so the family count grows exponentially
( 1.87K). Consequently the global sum exceeds :

                                        ()            0.587  >             0.415.                             (10)
                                : >0 2K()

This means the global gain bound cannot close the tail; a more refined analysis is needed.
    The resolution is that the contraction budget  applies per orbit, not globally. At each

Syracuse step, an orbit occupies a single residue class mod 2K and can be in the shadow of at
most one expanding family at each depth. Upon entering the shadow of a family , the orbit
remains for () Syracuse steps (consuming () bits of missed contraction), then exits. The
correct quantity is the per-step gain rate. The following three lemmas isolate the key analytic
ingredients; the theorem then combines them with exact finite computation.

Remark 7.21 (Interpretation). Under the Orbit Equidistribution Conjecture (Conjecture 8.2),
at each step the orbit's residue class mod 2K is approximately uniform. The probability of
entering the shadow of family  is 1/2K; upon entry, the orbit stays for  steps. The factor

1/ converts the per-encounter gain (which globally exceeds ) into a per-step rate ( /5),

reflecting mutual exclusion: an orbit in one shadow cannot simultaneously be in another.

Remark 7.22 (Why global gain exceeds ). The global sum (10) exceeds  because it treats all
shadows as simultaneously active. Under equidistribution, the expected halving per Syracuse
step is E[k] = 2, so E[] = -: the average family is contractive by exactly  per step. The
expanding tail (families with  > 0) contains the full positive fluctuation of this distribution,
which naturally exceeds the mean contraction. The per-orbit bound R avoids this overcounting
by amortising each encounter over its duration .

Remark 7.24 (Interpreting the admissibility condition). Condition (21) is substantially weaker
than exact equidistribution (K = 0 for all K). It requires only that the total variation errors
K be summable: for instance, polynomial decay K = O(K-) with  > 1, or any exponential
mixing rate, easily satisfies the bound. The 4.65× safety margin of Theorem 7.19 is what creates
this room: the tighter the bound on R, the larger the class of admissible orbit distributions.
The Orbit Equidistribution Conjecture (Conjecture 8.2) thus represents a sufficient condition,
not a necessary one; any orbit distribution whose depth-K total variation errors satisfy (21)
would close the phantom shadow tail bound equally well.

The census analysis isolates a single intrinsic arithmetic obstruction. For a phantom signature

The census constant is controlled not merely by the coefficient valuation s, but also by the
intrinsic near-return scale s. Indeed, if Es denotes the exact exit-class orbit after s steps and
devs := v2(Es - Fs()), then Es -  = (Es - Fs()) + (Fs() - ), and therefore, whenever
devs = s,

                                            v2(Es - ) = min(devs, s).

Thus the obstruction to improving the census bound is concentrated in the arithmetic size of
s.

Remark 7.30 (Intrinsic return controls the census). The census constant satisfies

                               Ce  2maxs min(s, K-Vs),

where Vs = k1 + · · · + ks. Since K - Vs decreases monotonically, the maximum is achieved when

quantity controlling the census constant.

7.8 The weighted self-overlap theorem

The following theorem shows that a large value of s forces a long weighted self-overlap of
the signature. This is the main structural bridge between the 2-adic census analysis and the
combinatorial problem of bounding cyclic pattern recurrence.

Theorem 7.31 (Large intrinsic near-return forces weighted self-overlap). Assume s = v2(Cs-

C)  m. Let r be the largest integer such that       r-1  kj  < m.    Then
                                                    j=0

           kj = kj+s mod  for all 0  j < r.

Equivalently,  and its cyclic rotation s agree entry-by-entry until the cumulative prefix val-
uation reaches m.

Proof. We use a carry-constant difference recursion. For any word  = (a0, . . . , aL-1) with carry
constant C( ) defined by F (n) = (3Ln + C( ))/2||, one has

                               C( ) = 3L-1 + 2a0 C( ),                             (23)

where   = (a1, . . . , aL-1). Since 3L-1 is odd and 2a0C( ) is even, it follows that v2(C( )) = 0
for all words of length  1.

    Now let ,  be two words of the same length L with first entries a0 and b0 respectively.
By (23),

                                      C( ) - C() = 2a0 C( ) - 2b0 C().

If a0 = b0, this factors as 2a0[C( ) - C()], so v2(C( ) - C()) = a0 + v2(C( ) - C()). If
a0 = b0, say a0 < b0, then C( ) - C() = 2a0[C( ) - 2b0-a0C()], and the bracket is odd (since
C( ) is odd and 2b0-a0C() is even), giving v2(C( ) - C()) = a0 = min(a0, b0).

    Apply this to  =  and  = s with v2(C( ) - C())  m. At each inductive step,
the cumulative prefix valuation has not yet reached m, so both first entries are strictly less
than m. If they differed, the valuation would equal min(a0, b0) < m, contradicting v2  m.
Hence a0 = b0, and we recurse with modulus reduced by a0. The induction terminates when
the cumulative prefix valuation reaches m, yielding the stated entry-by-entry agreement.

7.9 The counting reduction

Theorem 7.31 converts the 2-adic valuation problem into a combinatorial one. The following
definitions and proposition make this conversion precise.

Definition 7.32 (High-cancellation shift count). Define

                           N (m) := # s  {1, . . . ,  - 1} : s  m .

Definition 7.33 (Visible prefix at depth m). Let m  1, and let r = r(m) be the largest integer

such that  r-1  kj  <  m.  Define  the  visible  prefix  word  Pm  := (k0, k1, . . . , kr-1).
           j=0

Definition 7.34 (Cyclic occurrence count). Define

Occ(Pm) := # t  {0, . . . ,  - 1} : (kt, kt+1, . . . , kt+r-1) = (k0, k1, . . . , kr-1) ,

with indices taken modulo .

Proposition 7.35 (Counting reduction for high-cancellation shifts). For every m  1,

                                               N (m)  Occ(Pm) - 1.

Equivalently, every shift s with s  m produces a nontrivial cyclic occurrence of the visible
prefix Pm.

Proof. Fix m  1, and let r = r(m) be as in Definition 7.33. Suppose s  {1, . . . ,  - 1} satisfies
s  m. By Theorem 7.31, kj = kj+s mod  for all 0  j < r. Therefore the length-r block of
 starting at position s agrees with the prefix: (ks, ks+1, . . . , ks+r-1) = Pm. This defines an
injection

            { s  {1, . . . ,  - 1} : s  m }  { t  {0, . . . ,  - 1} : Pm occurs at t } \ {0},

since the shift s itself is the occurrence position.

Remark 7.36 (Significance of the counting reduction). The problem of bounding the number
of high-cancellation shifts N (m) is now reduced to a purely combinatorial question: how many
times can the visible prefix Pm recur cyclically in a phantom signature? Any structural theorem
showing that long visible prefixes cannot recur too often immediately yields a bound on N (m),
and hence on maxs s.

    For the seven known phantom families, the counting reduction is tight (i.e., N (m) =
Occ(Pm) - 1) for most values of m, verified computationally for all m up to the maximum
observed s. In a large-scale random study (3,000 families), the bound holds with 100% pass
rate over 22,067 test cases, and is tight 70% of the time.

Heuristic 7.37 (Carry-word autocorrelation bound). Computation over 5,000 random phan-
tom families supports max1s< s = O(log K). The data fits maxs s  1.2 log2 K. For sig-
natures with K/  log2 3, the visible prefix Pm is typically a run of r  m consecutive 1s.
Since consecutive 1-runs of length r occur approximately  · pr times (where p is the fraction
of 1-entries), the condition Occ(Pm)  2 forces r  log1/p()  1.24 log2 . Since   K/ log2 3,
this gives the O(log K) bound. Making this rigorous requires a structural theorem bounding
Occ(Pm) for phantom signatures.

                                                 33
7.10 The period-based occurrence bound

The counting reduction of Proposition 7.35 bounds N (m) by Occ(Pm) - 1. We now obtain a
sharper bound by analysing the least period of the visible prefix.

Lemma 7.38 (Two occurrences force a period). Suppose the visible prefix Pm = (k0, . . . , kr-1)
occurs cyclically in  at positions 0 and s, with 0 < s < r. Then s is a period of Pm:

                                       kj = kj+s for all 0  j < r - s.

Proof. By hypothesis, (ks, ks+1, . . . , ks+r-1) = (k0, k1, . . . , kr-1), where indices are taken mod-
ulo . For 0  j < r - s, both j and j + s lie in {0, . . . , r - 1}, so the indices on the left-hand
side fall within the prefix window. Reading off the j-th entry from the occurrence at position s
gives kj+s = kj.

Definition 7.39 (Least period of the visible prefix). Define pm to be the least period of the
word Pm:

                            pm := min p  1 : kj = kj+p for all 0  j < r - p .

Corollary 7.40 (Occurrence gaps are bounded below by the least period). If Pm occurs at
cyclic positions t0 < t1 < · · · < tq-1 in , then each gap gi := ti+1 - ti (indices mod q, with
wrap-around gap  - tq-1 + t0) satisfies gi  pm.

Proof. If some gap gi < pm, then by translating the two adjacent occurrences to positions
0 and gi (via cyclic reindexing of ), Lemma 7.38 produces a period gi < pm, contradicting
minimality.

Theorem 7.41 (Period-based occurrence bound). For every m  1,

      Occ(Pm)                               hence  N (m)   - 1.
                     ,                                          pm

                pm

Proof. By Corollary 7.40, consecutive occurrences are separated by at least pm. Since the
q := Occ(Pm) occurrences partition the cyclic interval {0, . . . ,  - 1} into q gaps each of size
 pm, summing gives q · pm  , whence q  /pm. The bound on N (m) follows from
Proposition 7.35.

Remark 7.42 (Significance). The bound N (m)  /pm - 1 converts the census problem
into the question: how fast does the least period pm grow with m? If pm  /f (m) for some
function f , then N (m)  f (m) - 1. In particular, any lower bound of the form pm  /poly(m)
would immediately yield a polynomial upper bound on N (m), and hence a polynomial census
bound.

Remark 7.43 (Computational verification). The full theorem package has been verified compu-
tationally: Lemma 7.38 passes all 4,556 relevant tests (known families and random signatures),
Corollary 7.40 passes 227,026 gap tests, and Theorem 7.41 passes 126,969 occurrence-bound
tests and 64,109 N (m)-bound tests, all with 100% success rate.

Proof sketch. Write  = C/D where D = 2K - 3p is odd. For two distinct sub-signatures

    Making this rigorous requires showing that phantom roots do not systematically belong to
exponentially sparse 2-adic subsets. Three possible routes toward a rigorous lower bound are:

                                                    35
(a) a genericity theorem for phantom roots (showing they avoid exponentially sparse 2-adic
     subsets);

(b) an algebraic constraint from the phantom equation (2K - 3) = C combined with  > 0;
     or

(c) a counting argument over all phantom signatures simultaneously.

7.12 Periodic-core factorization and defect analysis

The preceding subsections reduced the census bound to controlling the cyclic occurrence count
of a visible prefix. We now develop a finer algebraic tool: a periodic-core factorization that
isolates the contribution of the "tail" entries beyond a repeated block, and a defect expression
whose 2-adic valuation controls the census constants.

Proposition 7.48 (Periodic-core factorization [Supporting]). Let  =  q be a signature of

length , where  = (k0, . . . , kp-1) has length p, the repetition count q  1, and the tail  has

length t =  - qp. Write Kp =  p-1    ki  and  K   =  t-1   ki  for  the    weight  sums  of    and  
                              i=0                    i=0
respectively, and set K = qKp + K and D = 2K - 3, Dp = 2Kp - 3p. Then

               CDp - CpD = 2qKp CDp - CpD ,                                                    (24)

where C, Cp, C are the carry constants of ,  , and  respectively, and D = 2K - 3t.

Proof. Use the concatenation formula C() = 3|| C() + 2K() C() (Proposition 7.26). For
 =  q, iterating gives

                                              C = C( q) 3t + 2qKp C,

and for the pure repetition  q, the geometric sum yields

               C( q)  =       Cp  ·  2qKp  - 3qp  =  Cp ·  2qKp - 3qp
                                      2Kp  - 3p                         .

                                                               Dp

Substituting:

               CDp = Cp(2qKp - 3qp) 3t + 2qKp CDp
                      = Cp 3t · 2qKp - 3 + 2qKp CDp.

Meanwhile, CpD = Cp(2K -3) = Cp(2qKp+K -3) = Cp 2qKp 2K -Cp 3. Taking the difference:

CDp - CpD = 2qKp CDp - Cp 2K + Cp 3t = 2qKp CDp - Cp (2K - 3t) ,

and 2K - 3t = D, completing the proof.

Corollary 7.49 (Exact repetition gives exact root agreement). If  =  q (empty tail, t = 0),
then CDp - CpD = 0, and the 2-adic root satisfies  =  exactly.

Proof. With empty tail, C = 0 and D = 20 - 30 = 0, so the right-hand side of (24) vanishes.
Hence C/D = Cp/Dp, i.e.  =  .

Remark 7.50. The factorization cleanly separates the contribution of the periodic core from the
tail. The factor 2qKp on the right shows that increasing the repetition count does not change
the "tail defect" CDp - CpD; rather, it merely shifts it deeper into the 2-adic expansion.
This makes the 2-adic distance between  and the sub-root  entirely controlled by the tail
contribution.

                                              36
Definition 7.51 (Defect expression). For two signatures  = (a0, . . . , ap-1) and  = (b0, . . . , bt-1),
define

                                         E(, ) := C() D - C( ) D,

where D = 2K -3p, D = 2K -3t. The defect measures how far  and  are from agreement:
 -  = E(, )/(D D), and since D D is odd, v2( - ) = v2(E(, )).

Theorem 7.52 (First-mismatch valuation formula). Let  = (a0, . . . , ap-1) and  = (b0, . . . , bt-1)
be two signatures that agree on their first r entries (aj = bj for j < r) but differ at position r:
ar = br. Write Mr = a0 + a1 + · · · + ar-1. Then

         v2 E(, ) = Mr + min(ar, br).

Proof. Write  = C( )/D and  = C()/D, and consider the orbits

x0 =  ,  xj+1  =   3xj +                      1;      y0 = ,  yj+1     =  3yj +  1
                     2aj                                                    2bj   .

Since  is a fixed point of the composition F = Fap-1  · · ·  Fa0 (where Fa(x) = (3x + 1)/2a),
the orbit (xj) is the sequence of partial iterates.

Step 1: Shared entries preserve the valuation gap. Each map Fa(x) = (3x + 1)/2a satisfies
Fa(x) - Fa(y) = 3(x - y)/2a, so v2(xj+1 - yj+1) = v2(xj - yj) - aj (using v2(3) = 0). Since
aj = bj for j < r, iterating gives

         v2(xr - yr) = v2( - ) - Mr.

Step 2: At the mismatch, the valuation is min(ar, br). By definition of the signature entries,
v2(3xr + 1) = ar and v2(3yr + 1) = br. Writing 3xr + 1 = 2ar u and 3yr + 1 = 2br v with u, v
odd, we have xr - yr = (2ar u - 2br v)/3. Without loss of generality assume ar < br. Then

               xr  -  yr  =                   2ar (u  - 2br-ar v)

                                                        3

where u is odd and 2br-ar v is even, so u - 2br-ar v is odd. Since v2(3) = 0: v2(xr - yr) = ar =
min(ar, br).

Combining Steps 1 and 2: v2( - ) = Mr + min(ar, br). Since D D is odd, v2(E(, )) =
v2( - ) = Mr + min(ar, br).

Corollary 7.53 (Defect-tail mismatch bound). Returning to the setting of Proposition 7.48,
write  =  q where  = (a0, . . . , ap-1) and  = (b0, . . . , bt-1) with t  1. Let r be the index of
the first mismatch between  (cyclically extended) and : aj = bj for j < r and ar = br. Then
