         v2( -  ) = qKp + Mr + min(ar, br),

which grows linearly with the repetition count q.

Proof. By Proposition 7.48, v2(CDp-CpD) = qKp+v2(E(, )). By Theorem 7.52, v2(E(, )) =
Mr + min(ar, br). The result follows. Since Dp and D are both odd, v2( -  ) = v2(CDp -
CpD).

                                                  37
Remark 7.54 (Significance for the census bound). The first-mismatch formula gives a com-
putable, sharp lower bound on the 2-adic distance between  and any sub-root  : the distance
is controlled entirely by how long the tail  agrees with the periodic core  and the entry values
at the first point of disagreement. Together with the periodic-core factorization, this shows
that the intrinsic near-return valuation s (which controls the census constant Ce) decomposes
into a "repetition depth" qKp plus a "tail defect" Mr + min(ar, br). The former grows linearly
with the number of full periods in the rotation, while the latter is bounded by K + B, where
B = maxj kj. This decomposition is the key to upgrading the period-based occurrence bound
(Theorem 7.41) into a census bound: the number of high-cancellation shifts is controlled by how
many times the signature approximately repeats its own initial block, which in turn is bounded
by the period of the visible prefix.

7.13 The uniqueness-threshold bound on the census constant

We now combine the machinery of the preceding subsections to give a sharp, computable bound
on the census constant Ce in terms of a single combinatorial quantity: the uniqueness threshold
of the visible prefix.

Definition 7.55 (Uniqueness threshold). For a phantom signature  of depth K, define

               m := min m  1 : Occ(Pm) = 1 ,

where Occ(Pm) counts cyclic occurrences of the visible prefix Pm in . That is, m is the
smallest valuation depth at which the visible prefix becomes unique, it appears only at position 0
in the cyclic signature.

Theorem 7.56 (Uniqueness-threshold bound). For any phantom family (, ):

               max s  m - 1.

               1s<

Consequently,

               Ce  2m-1.                                                             (25)

Proof. Suppose for contradiction that s  m for some 1  s < . By the weighted self-overlap
theorem (Theorem 7.31), s  m implies that the rotation s agrees with  on positions
0, 1, . . . , r - 1, where r = |Pm| is the length of the visible prefix at depth m. In other words,

Pm occurs in the cyclic word  starting at position s (in addition to position 0). Hence
Occ(Pm)  2, contradicting the definition of m.

    For the census bound: by Remark 7.30, Ce  2maxs min(s,K-Vs)  2maxs s  2m-1.

Remark 7.57 (Tightness). The bound is tight to within an additive constant. For every known
phantom family, maxs s equals m - 1 or m - 2: the uniqueness threshold is essentially the
exact depth at which cyclic self-overlap ceases. Computational verification over 1,406 random
phantom-compatible signatures confirms the theorem with 100% pass rate, and the median gap
m - maxs s is just 2.

Remark 7.58 (Relationship to the gain formula). The uniqueness-threshold bound Ce  2m-1
characterises the census constant as a structural quantity. However, by Proposition 7.11, the
census excess Ce reflects a universal shift of the entire population (the intrinsic near-return),
not selective orbit tracking. Consequently, the gain formula is G = /2K (Proposition 7.13),
in which Ce does not appear.

    The uniqueness-threshold theorem remains valuable for understanding the autocorrelation
structure of phantom signatures, and the bound maxs s  m - 1 provides a sharp characteri-
sation of the depth at which cyclic self-overlap ceases.

               38
Heuristic 7.59 (Logarithmic uniqueness threshold). Extensive computation over 1,406 phantom-
compatible signatures with K ranging from 6 to 100 gives the empirical fit

                m  1.27 · log2 K + 1.24,

with m/ log2 K concentrated in the interval [1.1, 1.6]. This is consistent with a random-word
heuristic: for a cyclic word of length  over an effective alphabet of size B, a specific length-
r pattern occurs  /Br times, so uniqueness requires r  logB . Translating to valuation:
m  k¯ · logB , where k¯ = K/ is the mean entry.

    For all seven known phantom families, m  8 (achieved by ell8 with K = 10), and the ratio
m/ log2 K ranges from 0.93 (m10) to 2.41 (ell8). The following table gives the exact values:

Caveat. For pathological signatures such as  = (1, 1, . . . , 1, k), the uniqueness threshold can
grow as m =  = O(K) rather than O(log K), and Ce can be exponential in K. This does

not affect the gain bound (since Ce does not enter the gain formula), but it illustrates that the
logarithmic scaling of m is a property of generic phantom signatures, not a universal law.

Remark 7.60 (Structural summary). By the phantom universality theorem (Theorem 7.15),
every cyclic composition is a phantom family, and the family count grows exponentially (
1.87K). Nevertheless, the per-orbit gain rate theorem (Theorem 7.19) proves that the amortised
rate satisfies R = /( · 2K)  0.0893 <   0.415, with a 4.65× safety margin. This means
that the total expanding-family drift, amortised per step, is well within the contraction budget:
an equidistributed orbit loses more bits to halving than it gains from all phantom families
combined.

    The formal conditional proof of convergence is Theorem 8.14, which derives the burst­
gap hypotheses from the Orbit Equidistribution Conjecture. The phantom analysis provides
independent quantitative evidence that the equidistribution assumption is not artificially strong:
Corollary 7.23 shows that even summable approximate mixing ( K < 0.557) suffices.

Theorem 7.61 (Census-constant independence [Core]). The per-orbit phantom gain rate

R = R(K),       R(K) = 2-K              M (K, )  log2  3  -  K  ,
                                                             
           K 3              >K/ log2 3

is independent of the census constant Ce for every phantom family. Specifically, no term in the
series for R involves Ce: the gain contribution of each family is ()/2K, not () · Ce()/2K.

Proof. The proof proceeds in three steps.

    Step 1 (Universal census depth). By Proposition 7.11, at each intermediate step s of the
affine iteration with s < s, every lift x = e + 2Ku satisfies v2(T s(x) - ) = s exactly. The
census excess 2s is thus a uniform shift of the entire population at step s, it tracks proximity
to the rotated root s, not selective orbit behavior.

    Step 2 (End-of-cycle cancellation). After the full -step block, equation (9) gives F(x)- =
(3/2K)(x - ). Since 3 is odd and v2(x - )  K, the end-of-cycle valuation is v2(F(x) - ) =

                      39
v2(x - ) - K. The census ratio at the end of the cycle is therefore 1: the census excess
accumulated at intermediate steps is fully unwound by the closing contraction 2-K.

    Step 3 (Gain formula reduction). By Proposition 7.13, the gain contribution of family  is
G() = /2K with  =  log2 3 - K. The per-orbit rate R(K) = 2-K  M (K, )(log2 3 - K/)
is a sum of such contributions weighted by primitive necklace counts. Since Ce drops out of
each individual gain contribution (Step 2), it is absent from every term in R(K) and hence from
the total R = K R(K).

Remark 7.62 (Why this matters for the proof architecture). Theorem 7.61 is the formal
justification for classifying the carry-word analysis (Sections 7.7­7.13) as Supporting rather
than Core. The census constant Ce and its upper bound via the uniqueness threshold m
(Theorem 7.56) provide structural insight into the autocorrelation of phantom signatures, but
they are not load-bearing for the conditional proof: the chain

                               WMH -C-o-r-7-.23 Rµ <  -T-h-m--7.-19 convergence

passes through R(K), which depends only on the necklace counts M (K, ) and the drift , never
on Ce. Any future refinement of the census bound (e.g. proving m = O(log K) unconditionally)
would strengthen the supporting evidence but would not alter the core conditional implication.

8 Conditional convergence and reduction
     to orbit equidistribution

We now state the conditional convergence theorem and formulate the Orbit Equidistribution
Conjecture, which would supply the two open hypotheses required by the Burst-Gap Criterion.

8.2 The Orbit Equidistribution Conjecture

Conjecture 8.2 (Orbit Equidistribution Conjecture). For every odd n0, the sequence of residue
classes after applying Tg(n) ai = T i(n0) mod 2M is equidistributed modulo 2M , uniformly in M :

                   N  i=1

modulo 2M(N).

In particular, this implies fixed-modulus equidistribution (take M (N ) = M constant) and

provides the tail control needed to pass from truncated to full orbitwise means (see Theo-

rem 8.14).

8.3 The Weak Mixing Hypothesis

The Orbit Equidistribution Conjecture is stronger than necessary. Corollary 7.23 shows that
the phantom shadow tail bound closes under any orbit distribution whose total variation errors
are merely summable. We therefore formulate a quantitatively weaker hypothesis that suffices
for the full conditional reduction.

   · Polynomial: K = O(K-) with  > 1 (after rescaling the implicit constant so that

   · Exponential: K = O(rK) for any r < 1.

The 4.65× safety margin of Theorem 7.19 is what creates this room: the tighter the bound
on R, the larger the admissible class of orbit distributions.

Remark 8.11 (Why SOH is strictly the weakest condition). The Shadow-Occupancy Hypoth-

esis is strictly weaker than both WMH and observable-specific WMH, for three reasons. First,

SOH is one-sided: it controls only the positive quantity µorb,K(S+(K)), with no absolute value,

no lower bound, and no comparison to a target spatial average. Second, SOH makes no claim

about time-average matching: the orbit need not visit shadow residues at any specific frequency,

only at most a budgeted total mass. Third, SOH places no constraint at all on the residues

outside S+(K), which form the overwhelming majority of Z/2K.

The exact uniform baseline (computed via Möbius inversion through K = 500, with analytic

Chernoff tail) is

                        |S + (K )|    0.545878 + 1.7 × 10-9  <  0.5459,
                   K3 2K

so the SOH threshold 0.7095 leaves an absolute slack of  0.164 and an oversampling tolerance
of 0.7095/0.5459  1.30: a Collatz orbit may concentrate on shadow residues at up to 1.30×
the uniform rate on average and still satisfy SOH. This is a structurally easier target than the
K-based observable-specific WMH, which would require the same orbit to match the uniform
rate from below as well as from above.

Remark 8.12 (Relationship between WMH, observable-specific WMH, and SOH). Since K 
hK K  (log2 3-1) K, condition (28) is implied by (26) but may hold even when (26) does
not. The observable-specific condition requires only that the orbit's time-average of hK be close
to its spatial average, without any control on the full residue distribution. In turn, observable-
specific WMH implies SOH: from |Eµorb,K [hK ] - R(K)|  K and Eµorb,K [hK ]  (log2 3 - 1)-1 · 0
being trivial, one obtains the cruder one-sided budget µorb,K (S+(K))  (R + K )/(log2 3-
1) < /(log2 3 - 1), which is exactly (29). Since hK is supported on the sparse set of shadow
residues of expanding families (a fraction  2-K(1-1/ log2 3) of all classes at depth K), this is a

substantially weaker requirement. The hierarchy of open conditions is therefore:

           OEC = WMH = Obs-WMH = SOH =+=t=a=il=c=on=tr=ol Collatz

The first implication is Proposition 8.6; the second follows from K  (log2 3 - 1) K and
summation; the third is the inclusion just established; the fourth is Theorem 8.10, subject to

the orbitwise tail-control condition (30).

8.4 Conditional reduction theorems

We now state the conditional reduction in three forms, corresponding to the three levels of the
hierarchy above: from the original OEC (strongest hypothesis), from the WMH (primary open
target), and from the observable-specific WMH (weakest sufficient condition).

    Two auxiliary lemmas isolate the bounded-observable step (which is rigorous) from the
truncation passage (which remains an open step).

                                             43

the full averages and truncated averages is bounded by the corresponding tail averages. The

Theorem 8.14 (OEC implies convergence, modulo orbitwise truncation). Assume the Orbit

Proof. For each fixed truncation level K, the observables L(K) := min(L, K) and G(K) :=

The uniform-lift expectations are given by the exact modular laws proved earlier: gap lengths

Remark 8.15 (Status of Theorem 8.14). The bounded-observable step in the proof above is a
genuine, immediate consequence of the OEC. The unproved step is the orbitwise tail-vanishing
condition (30) needed to pass from truncated observables to the full means of Li and Gi.

    A fully rigorous version would require either:

1. a uniform integrability argument showing that equidistribution at depth M (N )  
   forces exponential tail decay for the orbit's gap and burst lengths, or

2. an independent orbitwise tail bound (e.g. from the Known-Zone Decay or a direct valuation
   argument).

Neither is provided here. Theorem 8.14 should therefore be read as a reduction theorem: the
full Collatz conjecture follows from orbit equidistribution plus orbitwise tail control, not from
orbit equidistribution alone.

    The same tail-control caveat applies to Theorems 8.16 and 8.8 below.

                                                    44
Theorem 8.16 (WMH implies conditional reduction, modulo orbitwise truncation [Core]).

total-variation discrepancy from uniformity. Then:

   1. The phantom-gain contribution remains strictly below the drift budget:

              Rµ  R + (log2 3 - 1) K <

   and is fully rigorous.

   2. For each fixed truncation level K, the bounded truncated observables L(K) and G(K) differ
      from their uniform expectations by at most O(K), by the standard total-variation bound.

   3. If the orbitwise tail-vanishing condition (30) (Lemma 8.13) holds for the unbounded burst
      and gap observables, then Hypotheses A and B of Theorem 8.1 follow and the orbit con-
      verges.

Proof. Part (1) is Corollary 7.23 combined with Theorem 7.19. Part (2) applies the TV duality

observables.  Part (3) follows from Lemma 8.13:  once (30) holds,  we obtain  1  Li  2 and

Remark 8.17 (The WMH as the primary open problem). Theorem 8.16 shows that the full
strength of the Orbit Equidistribution Conjecture is not required. The WMH is the quanti-
tatively weakest universal mixing condition that closes the conditional proof via the current
phantom-cycle framework. We therefore propose the Weak Mixing Hypothesis as the primary
open problem for completing the conditional programme:

              Collatz conjecture = WMH + tail control (30).

The bounded-observable part (phantom-gain control) is fully rigorous under WMH alone. The
remaining open step is the orbitwise truncation principle needed to pass from bounded to
unbounded observables. Any progress on the WMH, whether for all orbits, for a positive-density
set, or for specific structural classes, would constitute meaningful progress toward resolving the
conjecture.

Remark 8.18 (The distributional-vs-pointwise gap). The Scrambling Lemma proves a dis-
tributional result: if n is drawn uniformly from a residue class, the bits of T (n) are exactly
uniform. The conjecture requires a pointwise result: that each individual orbit equidistributes.

    This is a pointwise genericity question, analogous to proving that a given number is nor-
mal in base 2, not an almost-everywhere result. The map property (distributional uniformity)
does not automatically imply the orbit property (pointwise equidistribution), just as knowing
that the digit-frequency map preserves uniformity does not prove that a specific number like

  2 is normal.
    Tao's theorem [14] establishes almost-all convergence (in logarithmic density), which is a

probabilistic rather than pointwise statement. Both approaches thus face the same fundamental
barrier: the gap between distributional/almost-all and every-orbit results.

    The Weak Mixing Hypothesis softens this barrier: it does not require exact equidistribution
(K = 0), only summable errors ( K < 0.557). This is a quantitative relaxation that may be
more tractable than full pointwise equidistribution.

Remark 8.19 (What is known toward the conjecture). Several lines of evidence support the
Weak Mixing Hypothesis and the stronger Orbit Equidistribution Conjecture:

              45
1. The gap map preserves the uniform distribution (Scrambling Lemma, proved uncondi-
   tionally).

2. The Known-Zone Decay shows that the map is strongly information-erasing at the
   residue-class level: after M/2 applications, all dependence on the starting class is elim-
   inated (an algebraic fact, not an orbitwise mixing claim).

3. Almost-all equidistribution follows from Tao [14] (logarithmic density).

4. All empirical orbits tested (up to 22000) satisfy equidistribution within sampling noise.

9 Toward the Weak Mixing Hypothesis

Status of this section. The results in this section are not used in the proof of the condi-
tional reduction theorem (Theorem 8.1/8.16). They formulate the remaining bridge problem,
develop structural constraints on the depthwise discrepancy, and outline possible future theorem
programmes for proving the final orbitwise mixing input.

The core bottleneck. Everything in this paper reduces to a single question: does each
individual Collatz orbit mix sufficiently at moderate modular depths? No nontrivial orbitwise
mixing theorem is proved here. The algebraic and combinatorial framework is unconditional;
the sole remaining input for Route A is an orbitwise information-flow statement (the WMH or
one of its weakenings). This section develops structural constraints and possible attack routes,
but the reader should understand clearly: the core difficulty is untouched. Solving the
WMH is, in all likelihood, essentially as hard as the Collatz conjecture itself.

    The preceding sections reduce the phantom-cycle route to a single open orbitwise input. The
goal is no longer to prove exact orbit equidistribution; the robustness estimate of Corollary 7.23
shows that it suffices to control the cumulative depthwise discrepancy so that the induced
perturbation of the phantom-gain rate remains below the available margin  - R  0.326. The
Weak Mixing Hypothesis (Hypothesis 8.3) expresses this requirement as K(n0) < 0.557, and
the even weaker observable-specific WMH (Conjecture 8.7) requires only K(n0) < 0.326.

    This section develops structural results that constrain K from below and from above, lays
groundwork for proving one of the weak-mixing conjectures, and identifies a concrete three-
lemma programme for the final bridge.

9.1 Shadow sparsity

The phantom gain observable hK (Corollary 7.23) is nonzero only on residue classes that shadow
an expanding phantom family at depth K. The following proposition shows that these "expand-
ing shadow residues" are exponentially sparse.

depth K. Write min(K) := K/log2 3 + 1 and K := (min(K) - 1)/(K - 1). Then

Remark 9.3 (What would constitute progress). The framework reduces to proving that in-
dividual orbits do not persistently concentrate on the exponentially sparse expanding-shadow
residues. Concrete sufficient results include:

· Any bound of the form µ^K() = o(1) for Walsh characters  of Hamming weight 1 would,
   combined with the shadow sparsity and amplification cancellation, suffice to close the
   WMH (see Section 9.8).

· Any nontrivial upper bound on K(n0) for infinitely many K (e.g. K = O(K-) with
    > 1) would close the summability condition directly.

· A positive-density set of starting values satisfying the WMH would already extend the
   almost-all convergence results beyond Tao's logarithmic density.

None of these is proved here. We present them to make the target concrete, not to claim
proximity to a solution.

9.2 Repulsion-based shadow return time

The 2-adic repulsion property (Proposition 7.4) implies that once an orbit exits a phantom
shadow, it cannot re-enter the same shadow quickly. This provides a structural lower bound on
the inter-encounter gap.

Theorem 9.4 (Shadow return time [Supporting]). Let  be a primitive phantom family of

depth K and length , and let n0, n1, n2, . . . be a Syracuse orbit. Suppose nt enters the shadow
of  (i.e. nt   (mod 2K)) and exits after  steps at time t + . Then the orbit cannot re-enter

the shadow of  before time t +  + (K - 1). More precisely, if nt is the next shadow entry with
t > t + , then

                     t - t   + (K - 1).                               (33)

Proof. At entry, v2(nt - )  K. By the 2-adic repulsion property (Proposition 7.4), after one
full cycle of the block map:

v2(nt+ - ) = v2(nt - ) - K  K - K = 0.

                                  47
So at exit, the 2-adic alignment with  has decreased from  K to  0 (generically, v2(nt+-) =
0 when v2(nt - ) = K exactly).

    For re-entry, the orbit must again satisfy v2(nt - )  K. Each Syracuse step can increase
the 2-adic valuation v2(nj - ) by at most 1 (since a single halving step applies v2(3n + 1)  1,
and the 2-adic distance to  can gain at most one bit of alignment per step in the generic case
where nj is not in any related shadow).

    Starting from v2(nt+ - )  0, recovering v2  K requires at least K additional steps.
However, the exit step itself is step t + , and the first potentially aligning step is t +  + 1
(one cannot re-enter at t +  since v2 < K there). Therefore t  t +  + K - 1, giving
t - t   + K - 1.

Remark 9.5 (Amortisation consequence). Theorem 9.4 implies that the per-step gain from
family  is amortised over a gap of at least  + K - 1 steps (not just ). This provides an
alternative derivation of the 1/ amortisation in Theorem 7.19: the correct amortisation factor
is 1/( + K - 1)  1/, so the return-time bound only strengthens the existing per-orbit gain
rate. For families where K   (high-depth, short-length families with  just above K/log2 3),
the return-time gap is approximately K rather than , improving the amortisation by a factor
 log2 3.

Remark 9.7 (What the finite-depth reduction actually says). Proposition 9.6 does not elimi-

nate the need to control K(n0) for K > Kmax: the WMH summability condition is global in K.
What the reduction does do is isolate the difficulty: the first Kmax depths carry the bulk of
the WMH budget (via explicit constants), while the tail contribution from the shadow-sparsity
side is dominated by the analytic K>500 R(K)  6.22 × 10-7 of Theorem 7.19. An uncondi-
tional bound on K for K  Kmax, even a crude O(1) one, cannot by itself close the WMH;
summability over K remains the binding condition.

Remark 9.8 (Practical significance). The finite-depth reduction converts an infinite-series
condition into a finite verification problem. At each of the 53 depths K = 3, . . . , 55, one needs
to bound K (n0) = dTV(µorb,K (n0), µunif ), where µorb,K (n0) is the empirical distribution of
nt mod 2K along the orbit of n0. This converts an analytic question (summability of an infinite
series) into a combinatorial one (bounding finitely many TV distances).

    However, this "finite verification problem" is theoretical, not computational: each K(n0) is
defined as a lim sup over the infinite orbit, so it cannot be computed from any finite segment of
trajectory. The reduction makes the problem finite in the modular-depth variable K, but each
individual bound K < cK remains an infinite-orbit statement.

9.4 Hierarchical consistency

A key structural feature of the modular hierarchy is that an orbit's residue class modulo 2K
determines its class modulo 2K for all K < K. This creates a consistency constraint on how
badly an orbit can deviate at multiple depths simultaneously.

Proposition 9.9 (Hierarchical consistency [Supporting]). Let KK : Z/2K Z  Z/2KZ de-

note the natural projection (K  K). For any probability distribution µK on Z/2KZ, the push-
forward (KK)#µK is a probability distribution on Z/2KZ, and the total variation distances

satisfy

                           K  K for all K  K.                                          (36)

In particular, if µK = µorb,K (n0) is the orbit distribution at depth K, then K (n0) is monotone
non-decreasing in K: the orbit cannot be more uniform at a finer scale than at a coarser one.

                                         49
Proof. The projection KK is a deterministic function, so by the data-processing inequality
for total variation,

dTV (KK )#µK , (KK )#µunif  dTV(µK , µunif ) = K .

Since (KK)#µunif is the uniform distribution on Z/2KZ (because KK maps each of the
2K-K preimage classes uniformly), the left side equals K when µK is the orbit distribution.

Corollary 9.10 (Monotonicity constraint on the WMH sum). For any orbit starting at n0, the

WMH sum satisfies

                   Kmax

                         K (n0)  (Kmax - 2) 3(n0).                                     (37)

                   K =3

Hence the WMH requires 3(n0) < 0.557/(Kmax-2). For Kmax = 55, this gives 3(n0) < 0.0105.

Proof. By Proposition 9.9, K  3 for all K  3. Summing from K = 3 to Kmax yields (37).

Remark 9.11 (Interpretation). Corollary 9.10 shows that the WMH already imposes a strong
constraint at the coarsest depth: the orbit distribution of n0 mod 8 must be within 1.05% of
uniform in total variation. This bound applies to hypothetically non-convergent orbits (those
for which the WMH is the operative hypothesis): if the orbit never reaches 1, it generates
an infinite Syracuse sequence whose empirical distribution mod 8 the bound constrains. For
known convergent orbits, the Collatz conjecture holds directly and the WMH is not needed;
the transient distribution before convergence shows no systematic bias beyond sampling noise
in the orbits tested (n0 up to 260, with transients of several hundred odd iterates).

    The hierarchical constraint also means that the sequence K(n0) is non-decreasing, so the
WMH sum is dominated by the large-K terms. Combined with the finite-depth reduction
(Proposition 9.6), this shows that the critical battleground is the intermediate range K 
[20, 55]: below 20, the terms are small by empirical concentration; above 55, the tail is controlled
by Chernoff decay.

9.5 Depthwise discrepancy recurrence

The results above constrain K from the geometric structure of phantom shadows. The following
conjecture proposes a dynamical mechanism that would directly imply the WMH.

Conjecture 9.12 (Depthwise discrepancy recurrence). There exist constants   (0, 1), C > 0,
and  > 1 such that for every odd starting value n0 and every modular depth K  3:

                   K+3(n0)   K (n0) + C K-.                                            (38)

Remark 9.13 (Why step-3 increments). The increment of 3 corresponds to passing through one
full "burst" (a positive step with v2 = 1, consuming one depth unit) followed by the minimum
reload cycle. The s-invariant analysis (Section A.10) shows that each positive step decreases s
by exactly 1 and each negative step reloads by a geometrically distributed amount. The depth-
3 step is the shortest cycle that can include both a drain and a reload, making it the natural
"mixing time" unit for the 2-adic residue structure.

Proposition 9.14 (Recurrence implies WMH). If Conjecture 9.12 holds with  < 1, C > 0,
and  > 1, then the Weak Mixing Hypothesis holds for every odd n0.

Proof. Iterating (38) with step 3: for K = 3 + 3j,

                                                                    j-1

                   3+3j(n0)  j 3(n0) + C j-1-i (3 + 3i)-.

                                                                    i=0

                         50
Since 3  1 and  > 1 implies K3 K- < , the sequence 3+3j decays geometrically up to
a summable perturbation. Summing over all residue classes modulo 3 and using monotonicity

(K+r  K+3 for r = 1, 2, by Proposition 9.9):

                                 

                   K  3 3+3j

              K 3         j=0

                                                 

                     31        3     +   1  C         K -   .
                               -            -
                                                 K 3

The resulting sum is finite. For instance, with  = 0.9,  = 2, C = 0.1, and the empirical value

3  0.005:                            2
                                     6
           K    3  0.005  +  0.1  ·      = 3(0.05 + 1.645)  5.08.
                    0.1      0.1

This crude bound exceeds 0.557, but the actual error terms CK- are expected to be much
smaller than 0.1 · K-2. With C = 0.001 and  = 2:

           K  3 0.05 + 0.01 · 1.645 = 3 · 0.0664 = 0.199 < 0.557.

Remark 9.15 (Attack vectors for the recurrence). The depthwise recurrence (38) is the sharpest
form of the distributional-to-pointwise bridge problem. Eight approaches are currently under
investigation:

(A) Robustness corridor. The 2-adic repulsion (Proposition 7.4) and shadow return time
      (Theorem 9.4) together imply that orbit encounters with expanding shadows are sparse in
      time. If the "non-shadow" steps mix at rate  < 1, the recurrence follows with eK being
      the shadow contribution at depth K.

(B) Ergodic theory. The Scrambling Lemma (Theorem 5.1) proves distributional uniformity
      for the gap map. If the orbit visits a positive-density set of odd-to-odd steps (a weak
      recurrence hypothesis), then the empirical distribution converges to the invariant measure
      by the ergodic theorem.

(C) Strengthening Tao. Tao's theorem [14] proves almost-all convergence in logarithmic
      density. A quantitative refinement that gives explicit mixing rates for the orbit distribution
      modulo 2K would directly yield the recurrence.

(D) Lattice-path combinatorics. The per-orbit gain rate (Theorem 7.19) was proved via
      lattice-path enumeration (necklace counts). A refinement that tracks the correlation be-
      tween consecutive shadow encounters (rather than just the rate) could yield the contractive
      factor .

(E) Generating functions. The Dirichlet series for the gain rate, K R(K) K-s, has ana-
      lytic structure governed by the Chernoff exponent D. If the corresponding "orbit Dirichlet
      series" (replacing the uniform expectation with the orbit empirical measure) inherits this
      analytic structure, Tauberian theorems could yield the exponential mixing rate needed for
      the WMH.

(F) Information-theoretic channel capacity. The Syracuse map, viewed as a noisy chan-
      nel from the current residue class mod 2K to the next, destroys  2 bits of input in-
      formation per odd-to-odd step (generically  3; by Known-Zone Decay, Theorem 6.1).
      After K/3 steps, the channel capacity for residue information at depth K drops to
      zero: the output residue class is independent of the input (conditionally on the odd-to-
      odd sequence). The alignment-renewal question (Conjecture 9.22) can be restated in this

                                     51
       language: after the channel capacity drops to zero, does the deterministic dynamics regen-
       erate mutual information between the orbit position and the gain-support set S+(K)? The
       empirical answer is no: mutual information between nt mod 2K and nt+d mod 2K drops
       to near zero by d  K/3 at low depth, and the amplification factor AK  2.4 across all
       tested orbits indicates that post-exhaustion residues are approximately uniform over the
       gain-support. A rigorous channel-capacity bound formalising this observation would close
       the alignment-renewal conjecture.

 (G) Walsh spectral diffusion. The Walsh­Fourier decomposition (Section 9.8) decomposes
       K into Hamming-weight bands. The orbit's spectral content at each band decays ge-
       ometrically (Observation 9.48), and Proposition 9.52 shows that formalising this decay
       (Conjecture 9.50) would imply the Amplification Hypothesis.

 (H) Orbit Walsh equidistribution. Weyl-type bounds on the Walsh exponential sums
       S(T ) = T -1 t (nt mod 2K ) would imply the Spectral Diffusion Conjecture via clas-
       sical equidistribution theory. This connects the problem to the extensive literature on
       exponential sum estimates for arithmetic sequences.

  (I) Odd-skeleton drift mixing. The Syracuse drift signal di = log2 3-vi +log2(1+1/(3ni))
       has negative mean and rapidly decaying autocorrelation (Observation 9.59). If Known-
       Zone Decay implies summable autocorrelation ( |(k)| < ), then Ibragimov's CLT
       gives xT  -, forcing a negative crossing. This reduces Collatz to a single mixing
       estimate on the odd skeleton, bypassing the gain-budget framework entirely.

Remark 9.16 (Fundamental computational limitation). Any attempt to verify the WMH com-
putationally faces an inherent limitation: if an orbit starting at n0 converges to 1 (as the Collatz
conjecture asserts), its transient has finite length, typically O(log n0) steps. For the WMH at
depth K, meaningful entropy estimates require (2K) orbit samples, yet the transient length
is at most logarithmic in the starting value. At K = 12 (where 2K-1 = 2048), even the longest
known transient (from n0 = 837 799, with 196 odd steps) is too short to fill the residue classes.

    This is not merely a practical limitation; it reflects a structural asymmetry. The WMH is
a statement about orbits that might, in principle, fail to converge; for convergent orbits, the
empirical distribution eventually concentrates at the fixed point 1  1 (mod 2K) and cannot
satisfy the mixing hypothesis. Thus the WMH is meaningful only during the transient regime,
which is precisely the regime where empirical data is scarce.

Remark 9.17 (The transfer operator is absorbing, not mixing). Let TK denote the trans-
fer operator of the Syracuse map on odd residues modulo 2K: for a probability vector µ on
(Z/2K Z)odd, (TK µ)(a) = T (b)=a µ(b). Computation for K = 3, . . . , 8 reveals that TK is nilpo-
tent (all non-leading eigenvalues are zero): the spectral gap equals 1, and iterated application of
TK absorbs the distribution onto the periodic orbits of the map mod 2K in finitely many steps.

    This means no single-depth transfer operator produces mixing. The Syracuse map at fixed
depth K contracts distributions toward absorbing cycles, driving the system away from unifor-
mity. The mixing property required by the WMH must therefore arise from the composition of
operators at different effective depths, precisely the mechanism captured by Known-Zone Decay
(Theorem 6.1), which proves that alternating odd-to-odd steps at varying valuations destroy
residue information.

    This reframes the alignment-renewal conjecture as a problem in the theory of products
of non-commuting matrices: if the orbit generates a "sufficiently generic" sequence of single-
step operators Tk1, Tk2, . . . (where ki = v2(3ni + 1)), does the product TkN · · · Tk1 contract
toward the uniform distribution? Furstenberg's theorem guarantees contraction under strong
irreducibility and proximality conditions on the matrix ensemble. Verifying these conditions
for the Syracuse operator family is a concrete (though non-trivial) research target that would
resolve the alignment-renewal conjecture.

                                                             52
9.6 Gain-observable recurrence

The depthwise recurrence (Conjecture 9.12) controls the full total variation K. A sharper and
potentially more provable target controls only the phantom gain observable.

Conjecture 9.18 (Gain-observable recurrence). There exist constants   (0, 1), C > 0, and
 > 1 such that for every odd starting value n0 and every depth K  3:

K+3(n0)   K (n0) + C K-,                                          (39)

where K(n0) is the phantom gain discrepancy from Conjecture 8.7.

Remark 9.19 (Gain-observable vs. full-TV recurrence). Since K  (log2 3 - 1) K, the gain-
observable recurrence (39) is implied by the depthwise recurrence (38) (with rescaled con-
stants). But (39) may be strictly easier to prove because hK is supported on the gain-support
set S+(K) (Proposition 9.1), which is asymptotically exponentially sparse (2-(K) fraction of
residue classes for large K). The recurrence need only track the orbit's behavior on this set,
rather than controlling the full residue distribution. By the same iteration argument as Propo-
sition 9.14, Conjecture 9.18 implies the observable-specific WMH (Conjecture 8.7), which in
turn implies the Collatz conjecture modulo the orbitwise tail-control condition (Theorem 8.8).

9.7 First lemmas toward the recurrence

The recurrence conjectures above break naturally into three component lemmas, each targeting
a specific mechanism.

Lemma 9.20 (Repulsion suppresses prolonged phantom trapping [Supporting]). For every
phantom family  of depth K and length , if an orbit shadows  at precision m  K (i.e.
v2(nt-)  m), then after at most m/K full block iterations, the orbit is expelled to precision
m mod K  K - 1.

Proof. By repeated application of the 2-adic repulsion (Proposition 7.4): after j full -step
blocks,

                                             v2 nt+j -  = m - jK.

This equals m - jK  K precisely when j  (m - K)/K = m/K - 1. At j = m/K, the
precision drops to m - m/K · K = m mod K  K - 1. At this point, v2 < K, so the orbit is
no longer in the shadow of .

Lemma 9.21 (Known-zone memory loss [Supporting]). After M/2 odd-to-odd steps, the
residue information at depth M is fully exhausted. More precisely, the Known-Zone Decay
(Theorem 6.1) implies that ZM/2 = 0: the orbit's position modulo 2M at step M/2 is
independent of its initial position modulo 2M , conditionally on the odd-to-odd sequence of the
Collatz orbit. Consequently, any residual discrepancy at depth M persists only through renewed
alignment events: occasions where the orbit re-enters a depth-M shadow after the known zone
has been exhausted.

Proof. By Theorem 6.1, the known-zone width satisfies Zk+1  max(0, Zk - 2) at each odd-
to-odd step, starting from Z0 = M . After M/2 steps, ZM/2  M - 2M/2  0. Hence
Z = 0: the Scrambling Lemma produces a uniformly random residue class modulo 2M at this
step, independent of the starting class.

    Any subsequent discrepancy M > 0 must therefore arise from correlation introduced after
the known zone was exhausted; that is, from renewed alignment events where the orbit enters
a shadow at depth M and accumulates a non-uniform residue distribution.

                                53
Conjecture 9.22 (Summable alignment-renewal bound). For every orbit starting at odd n0
and every depth K  3, the total discrepancy contribution from renewed depth-K alignment

events satisfies

                                        Krenew(n0)  C K-                  (40)

for constants C > 0 and  > 1 independent of n0.

Remark 9.23 (Amplification decomposition). Conjecture 9.22 admits a natural decomposition

that identifies the irreducible subproblem. After the known zone is exhausted at depth K, any

renewed discrepancy arises only when the orbit revisits the gain-support set SK (the set of
residues modulo 2K that contribute non-zero phantom gain). By Proposition 9.1, the uniform
measure of SK is exponentially small: µunif (SK ) = 2-(K). Define the amplification factor

                               AK (n0)  =  Prorb(fresh block hits SK ) ,
                                                     µunif (SK )

measuring how much more likely the orbit is to hit gain-relevant residues than a uniform random
walk. Then the renewal discrepancy satisfies

                              Krenew(n0)  AK (n0) · µunif (SK ) · (log2 3 - 1).

Conjecture 9.22 therefore reduces to showing that AK = O(K) for some   0: the orbit's
hitting probability of the gain-support exceeds the uniform prediction by at most a polynomial
factor. Since µunif (SK ) = 2-(K) (Proposition 9.1), even polynomial amplification yields the
required summable K- decay. This isolates the final bottleneck: bounding the amplification
factor AK is the irreducible subproblem of the conditional programme.

Remark 9.24 (Computational evidence for the amplification bound). We computed the em-
pirical amplification factor AK(n0) for 13 starting values n0  {27, 31, 41, 97, 127, 255, 447, 639,
703, 871, 6171, 77031, 837799} at depths K = 3, . . . , 10. For each orbit, the transient portion
(before reaching 1) was used to estimate Prorb(n mod 2K  S+(K)), where S+(K) is the gain-
support (Proposition 9.1).

    Across all 104 measurements, the amplification factor satisfies AK  2.4, with mean values
ranging from 0.84 (at K = 3) to 1.79 (at K = 8). No systematic growth of AK with K is
observed: the data is consistent with AK = O(1) (bounded amplification), which is substantially
stronger than the polynomial bound O(K) needed for Conjecture 9.22.

    We also note that the gain-support fraction |S+(K)|/2K-1 oscillates between approximately
0.19 and 0.50 for K = 3, . . . , 12 (with even K giving smaller fractions), and does not exhibit
the exponential decay predicted by Proposition 9.1 until much larger K. The shadow-sparsity
exponent D/2  0.025 is extremely slow: the bound becomes non-trivial only for K  40.
At the depths dominating the WMH sum (K = 3, . . . , 20), the gain-support is a substantial
fraction of odd residues, and the summability of R(K) at these depths relies on the per-family
gain (log2 3 - K/) being small, not on support sparsity.

Corollary 9.26 (No uniform set-level amplification bound). A bound of the form WK  C · BK

with K-independent constant C, where WK is the weighted gain contribution and BK the set-
level contribution from S+(K), is impossible: K  C fails for all C.

Remark 9.27 (Consequence for the amplification route). Proposition 9.25 does not invalidate

the amplification programme, but it does sharpen the requirement: any closing argument must
control the orbit's distribution within S+(K) (where inside the positive-gain set the orbit places
its mass), not merely the probability of hitting S+(K) as a whole. Bounding the amplification

factor AK alone is insufficient unless accompanied by a profile-weighted version that accounts
for the gain variation across S+(K).

Proposition 9.39 (No algebraic contraction [Supporting]). The algebraic extension structure
provides no contraction mechanism in any of the following senses:

1. -class Birkhoff contraction. The Birkhoff contraction coefficient of the -class transition
   matrix satisfies B = 1 for all K  5 and all s  1. This is because the transition matrix
   is a band of width s in a space of dimension K: rows corresponding to 1 and 2 with
   |1 - 2| > s have disjoint column support, giving zero overlap.

2. Full residue-class spectral gap. The extension map a  a + e · 2K is a translation
   in Z/2K+sZ. All singular values of the resulting transition matrix are equal (= 2-s/2), so
   there is no spectral gap and no preferred decaying direction.

3. Gain-observable harmonicity. The pullback of the depth-(K+s) gain observable through
   the algebraic transition exactly equals the depth-K gain observable: T hK+s = hK with
   zero residual (verified numerically for K  8, s = 3). The algebraic operator preserves
   gain discrepancy exactly.

                                 58
Proof. (1) For the s-step extension, the transition probability from parent -class  to child

-class  is    s  /2s  if  0     -        s  and   zero     otherwise  (by      Extension  Independence,
            -

Proposition 9.33). For |1 - 2| > s, the rows indexed by 1 and 2 have disjoint support,

so  min(T1,, T2,) = 0 and B = 1 - 0 = 1. Since K  5 > 3 = s (for the standard 3-step

block), such a pair always exists.

(2) The 2s children of parent a are {a + e · 2K : 0  e < 2s}, forming a coset of the subgroup

2KZ/2K+sZ. Each row of the transition matrix is a uniform distribution on a distinct coset.

The singular value decomposition reflects this group structure: all singular values equal 2-s/2.

(3) By linearity and the harmonic property (Corollary 9.29),            e  1   hK+s(a + e · 2K )  =  hK (a)
                                                                           2s

for every parent residue a. This is T hK+s = hK exactly.

Remark 9.40 (Implications of no algebraic contraction). Proposition 9.39 retires the entire
family of proof strategies based on algebraic contraction: Birkhoff projective metrics, spectral
gaps of transfer operators, operator-norm decay of the inherited bias, and cluster-decay argu-
ments relying on algebraic transport. Any contraction in the orbit's distribution across depths
must come from the orbit's deterministic selection, not from the algebraic extension structure.

Remark 9.41 (Density-model evidence [Supporting]). Numerical computation reveals that

the orbit's amplification ratio at depth K is governed by a single parameter: the odd-step
density  = limT  T -1#{t  T : nt odd}. Under the density model, the -distribution at
depth K is approximately 1 + Bin(K-1, ), giving amplification ratio

                      ampK ()       =  Pr[Bin(K -1,  )       K/log2   3  -  1]  .
                                       Pr[Bin(K -1,          K/log2   3  -  1]
                                                     1  )
                                                     2

The density-predicted gain rate Rµ() = K ampK() R(K) remains well within budget for all
convergent densities:

                                 Rµ()  0.142 for all   0.63  1/log2 3,

compared with   0.415 (margin  65%). For typical orbit densities   0.58­0.60, the
predicted Rµ  0.12­0.13.

    This suggests that the 4.65× safety margin of Theorem 7.19 absorbs the entire density effect.
The density model is not yet a theorem. Moreover, using  < 1/log2 3 as input is essentially
assuming what must be proved: the density condition is equivalent to negative mean drift,
which is the convergence condition itself. The density model's value is therefore structural,
not evidential: it identifies the orbit's odd-step density as the dominant parameter controlling
amplification, and shows that the safety margin absorbs the entire density effect if convergence
holds.

Since h¯K+3 = hK , the inherited component of the discrepancy K+3 is

                          h¯K+3 d(µ - uK ) = hK d(µK - uK ) = K ,

                      K

because µK+3 = µK when both are time-averaged orbit distributions. The recurrence there-

fore takes the form

                                       K+3  K + FK ,                                                 (45)

with FK =   rK+3 d(µK+3 - uK+3)          3  log2  3  ·     K+3,  where   K     :=  µK - uK TV.       The
                                         2

inherited factor is  = 1, not (K-4)/(K-1): the norm contraction of Proposition 9.28 bounds

the operator norm but not the integrated value.

The contraction needed to close the programme must therefore come from the distribution

side (showing K decays), not the observable side. However, Proposition 9.39 shows that the

                                            59
algebraic extension structure provides no contraction mechanism: Birkhoff coefficient B = 1,

all singular values are equal, and the gain observable is exactly harmonic. This retires any

strategy based on operator-norm contraction of K.

    The remaining viable path is direct summability: show K K(n0) <  - R directly. Since
R(K) decays exponentially (Bahadur­Rao), it suffices to show the orbit's amplification factor

AK  grows subexponentially.  Note that the structural  amplification AKcross  grows  as    1  e0.034K
                                                                                           2

(Remark 9.35), but this applies to the uniform measure, not to any particular orbit. The

orbit visits only O(log n0) residues per depth, and extension independence (Proposition 9.33)

guarantees the algebraic structure is unbiased. The density model (Remark 9.41) provides

strong numerical evidence that this path closes: for any fixed odd-step density  < 1/log2 3, the
predicted gain rate Rµ()  0.142 < .

Remark 9.43 (The three-lemma programme). The three results above outline a concrete
programme for proving the gain-observable recurrence (Conjecture 9.18):

1. Repulsion trapping (Lemma 9.20, proved): shadow encounters are bounded in duration;
   each encounter at precision m lasts at most m/K blocks.

2. Known-zone memory loss (Lemma 9.21, proved): after M/2 odd-to-odd steps, the or-
   bit's residue information at depth M is erased; subsequent discrepancy requires renewed
   alignment.

3. Summable alignment renewal (Conjecture 9.22, open): the renewal contribution decays as
   K- with  > 1.

If (3) is established, the gain-observable discrepancy satisfies (45): K+3  K + FK, where
the fresh term FK decays as K- from the alignment-renewal bound. Telescoping gives K 
6 + jK Fj, bounded provided  > 1. The repulsion lemma ensures that each alignment
event has bounded duration, preventing the orbit from being "trapped" near a phantom root
indefinitely.

    This remains the most promising rigorous path toward the WMH, now that operator-norm
contraction of the TV distance is excluded by Proposition 9.39. The density-model evidence
(Remark 9.41) suggests the quantitative margin is large enough to absorb typical orbit biases
without a contraction mechanism.

9.8 Spectral analysis of the gain observable

The three-lemma programme and density model locate the difficulty: contraction must come
from the orbit's distributional evolution, not from the algebraic operator. What has been
missing is a mechanism that makes this precise. The Walsh­Fourier decomposition on Z/2KZ
provides one.

Walsh­Fourier setup. The group Z/2KZ is a finite abelian 2-group with character group
generated by Walsh functions

                                                               K -1

            (a) = (-1)a,, a,  = ai i mod 2,

                                                                i=0

where ai, i are binary digits. The Walsh­Hadamard transform of f : Z/2KZ  R is f^() =
2-K a f (a) (a).

Proposition 9.44 (Spectral decomposition of gain [Supporting]). For any orbit measure µK

on Z/2K Z,  K = hK (a) µK (a) = 2K h^K () µ^K ().

                                                                                              (46)

                             a                         

                                60
Grouping by Hamming weight w = hw() gives the band-by-band decomposition

                          K

K = 2K h^K (0) µ^K (0) +              2K h^K () µ^K () .                           (47)

                          w=1 hw()=w

The first term is the DC (density) contribution; each subsequent band isolates the gain from
modes of a fixed spectral complexity.

Proof. This is the Parseval­Plancherel identity on Z/2KZ, which is a Pontryagin-dual group
isomorphic to itself. The Hamming-weight grouping follows from the partition { : hw() = w}
of the frequency domain.

    The spectral decomposition replaces the global question "is the orbit equidistributed mod 2K?"
with a band-by-band question: "does the orbit's Walsh content at Hamming weight w decay
with K?" This is strictly weaker: full equidistribution requires µ^K()  0 for every non-DC
mode, while the spectral gain formula only needs this for modes where h^K() is non-negligible.

Observation 9.45 (Spectral concentration of hK). Numerical computation for K = 3, . . . , 12
reveals:

   1. The DC component ( = 0) carries 25­31% of total Walsh power  |h^K()|2.

   2. The K modes with hw() = 1 carry 42­47% of total power, all sharing the same coefficient
       (by bit-permutation symmetry of hK).

3. Together, hw = 0 and hw = 1 account for 70­78% of total power.

4. By K = 12, the top 10% of modes carry 94% of total energy; the positive-gain signal hK+
   is even sparser, with active modes decreasing from 100% at K = 5 to 2% at K = 12.

    The concentration means that to bound K, it suffices to control µ^K() at low Hamming
weights, a much weaker requirement than total-variation equidistribution.

Remark 9.46 (Connection to the Krawtchouk basis). The Krawtchouk polynomials Kw(; K),
the spherical harmonics of the Hamming scheme, form the natural eigenbasis for functions that
depend primarily on hw(a). Since hK(a) does, the -class structure depends on hw(a), the Walsh
spectrum h^K() is (exactly for odd K, approximately for even K) constant on each Hamming
shell { : hw() = w}. This upgrades (47) from a grouping to a structural decomposition: each
band has a single spectral weight h^w = h^K() for all hw() = w, reducing the gain to K + 1
numbers.

Spectral diffusion. The central new phenomenon is that the orbit's Walsh coefficients decay
with depth, even though the algebraic transition operator is an isometry (Proposition 9.39).

Definition 9.47 (Spectral content at weight w). For an orbit measure µK on Z/2KZ,

Sw(K) =  1                    |µ^K ()|2.

         K

         w hw()=w

Observation 9.48 (Spectral diffusion). The long-run orbit measure (which is dominated by the
convergence cycle {1, 2, 4}) shows rapid spectral decay: Sw(K)  Cw · 2-wK with 1  1.5­
2.0 and R2 > 0.98. This decay has a simple explanation: the cycle's three residues form a
progressively smaller fraction of Z/2KZ as K grows.

    The transient orbit (before reaching 1) shows slower decay. For long-transient orbits (n0 
{837799, 8400511, 63728127} with T = 525­950 transient steps), S1(K) decreases from  0.03
at K = 4 to  0.016 at K = 10, a rate of 1  0.1­0.3. The transient orbit's spectral
content at hw = 1 is 10­17× larger than for a random sequence of equal length (measured at

z > 20 standard deviations), confirming that the orbit retains structured spectral bias during

the transient.

                          61
Remark 9.49 (Mechanism: orbit-driven, not operator-driven). Spectral diffusion does not
come from the transition operator (which is an isometry by Proposition 9.39). It comes from
the orbit's own ergodic properties: each odd-to-odd step destroys  3 bits of residue information
(Known-Zone Decay, Theorem 6.1), which in Walsh language contracts the orbit's low-frequency
Walsh coefficients. Extension independence (Proposition 9.33) ensures that the algebraic ex-
tensions do not re-inject spectral bias: suffix compositions are exactly Bin(s, 1/2), which is
spectrally neutral across all Hamming-weight bands. This is the mechanism the three-lemma
programme (Remark 9.43) was seeking: Known-Zone Decay provides the "memory loss" in
each spectral band, extension independence prevents "re-injection," and the band-by-band sum
converges if the spectral content at each band decays.

Conjecture 9.50 (Spectral Diffusion Conjecture). For every convergent Collatz orbit with
odd-step density  < 1/log2 3, there exists C() > 0 such that for every w  1,

                              Sw(K)  C() · 2-wK                                       (48)

with w > 0 depending only on w.

Remark 9.51 (Circularity warning). Conjecture 9.50 assumes the orbit has odd-step density
 < 1/log2 3, which is itself equivalent to the orbit having negative mean drift. This is not
circular in a logical sense (the conjecture is stated as a conditional), but any attempt to use the
conjecture to prove convergence must establish the density condition independently, or work
with a weaker formulation that does not presuppose it. The density condition  < 1/log2 3 is
the critical drift threshold: precisely the condition that orbit convergence requires. Assuming
it as a hypothesis and then deriving convergence is valid only as a structural reduction, not as
evidence of proximity to a proof.

Proposition 9.52 (Spectral diffusion implies amplification [Supporting]). Conjecture 9.50
implies the Amplification Hypothesis (Hypothesis 9.37) with quantitative margin.

Proof. By (46) and Cauchy­Schwarz,

                                          w

(Remark 9.36), 2K hK 12 ·  K  = O(1).     If the spectral diffusion conjecture holds, each term

Walsh mixing rate. Known-Zone Decay (Theorem 6.1) erases  3 bits of residue information
after applying Tg(n). In Walsh language, this means the orbit's Walsh characters decorrelate
rapidly.

Observation 9.53 (Walsh character mixing (empirical)). The following is observed computa-
tionally, not proved. For transient orbits (n0  {837799, 8400511}, T  525), the autocorrelation
of the Walsh character (nt mod 2K) at lag K/3 satisfies |autocorr| < 0.13 for all tested
K  {6, 8, 10} and all Hamming weights w  1. The mixing rate is bounded well away from 1:

                                          62
        w |autocorr|           at lag K/3

        1 0.065­0.126 (decreasing with K)

        2 0.030­0.049             (stable)

        3 0.034­0.049             (stable)

         4 0.032­0.061            (stable)

This confirms that after K/3 orbit steps, the Walsh characters are essentially uncorrelated,
quantifying Known-Zone Decay in spectral language.

Martingale structure. The harmonic property (Corollary 9.29) provides the martingale
structure: T hK+s = hK means the "conditional expectation of future gain equals current gain."
If the orbit's distributional evolution provides a downward drift making K a supermartingale,
Doob's convergence theorem gives K K <  directly. The Walsh mixing rate (Observa-
tion 9.53) provides the mechanism for such a drift: rapid decorrelation of Walsh characters
implies the orbit's spectral bias dissipates between consecutive depth levels.

Exponential sum bridge. The gain can be rewritten as a Walsh exponential sum along the

orbit:                                   T

        K = h^K () S(T ),  S(T )  =  1       (nt  mod  2K ).               (49)
                                     T
                                        t=1

Weyl's inequality and van der Corput's method bound such sums for sequences with sufficient
"non-resonance." Numerical measurement (n0 = 27, K = 8, T = 500,000) shows |S| decreasing
with Hamming weight: 0.15 at hw = 1, 0.07 at hw = 2, 0.09 at hw  3. If a Weyl-type
bound |S(T )|  C/T  could be established, the Spectral Diffusion Conjecture would follow
from classical equidistribution theory. This provides a bridge from the spectral framework to
the extensive literature on exponential sums of arithmetic sequences.

Remark 9.54 (The implication chain). The spectral results assemble into:

        Weyl bounds on orbit Walsh sums = Spectral Diffusion (Conj. 9.50)
                           = Amplification Hyp. 9.37 = WMH.

This chain adds a new leftward entry point: the proof architect can now attack either the
alignment renewal (Conjecture 9.22) from the three-lemma programme, or the Spectral Diffusion
Conjecture from the Walsh framework, or Weyl-type exponential sum bounds from analytic
number theory. All three routes converge to the same open input. Of the three, the spectral
route is arguably the most promising mathematically, because it connects the Collatz problem
to the well-developed theory of exponential sums and Walsh analysis. However, no nontrivial
bound on |S(T )| has been proved for Collatz orbits, and the existing measurements (two orbits,
one modulus) are insufficient to establish even a conjectural rate.

Remark 9.55 (Spectral diagnostics: tightness and cancellation). Two diagnostics expose the

structure and limits of the spectral framework.

    Cauchy­Schwarz tightness. The band-by-band Cauchy­Schwarz bound in Proposition 9.52

overestimates |K| by a factor of  500­1000× at K = 6­8 (tightness ratio  0.001). The slack
comes from massive cancellation between Hamming-weight bands: the individual band contri-
butions K(w) = 2K hw()=w h^K ()µ^K () are each O(2K ) in magnitude, but their alternating
signs produce a sum K = O(1). Any proof via the spectral framework must therefore exploit
the sign structure of h^K, not merely bound each band separately.

    Bit-position parity profile. The orbit's hw = 1 Walsh coefficients have an informative
internal structure. Since µ^K (2j) = 1 - 2j, where j = T -1#{t : bit j of nt mod 2K is 1}, the
K single-bit characters carry the orbit's bit-by-bit parity profile. Numerical measurement for

                           63
transient  orbits  shows  |0  -  1  |    0.12,  |1  -      1  |      0.09,  |2   -  1   |    0.06,  and     |j  -  1  |  <  0.02
                                 2                         2                        2                              2

for j  6 across all tested orbits. The rapid decay of the profile means only the lowest  6 bit

positions carry significant parity bias; higher bits are effectively unbiased. This concentrates

the gain's spectral sensitivity on a fixed number of bit positions, independent of K.

Remark 9.56 (What the spectral framework does not add). The spectral framework reformu-
lates the open question but does not resolve it. The fundamental problem: whether an orbit can
sustain   1/log2 3 indefinitely, becomes "can an orbit sustain non-decaying Walsh coefficients
at hw = 1?" This is a different language for the same question. The Cauchy­Schwarz bound
in Proposition 9.52 is far from tight (Remark 9.55), and closing the gap requires structural
cancellation arguments that the current framework does not provide. The framework provides
mechanism and structure, not a free theorem.

9.9 The odd-skeleton crossing route

The spectral analysis of the gain observable (Section 9.8) operates on the residue-class depth
hierarchy Z/2KZ. A complementary approach works on the orbit's time domain: define a drift
signal from the Syracuse (odd-to-odd) map and reformulate convergence as a negative-crossing
problem.

Even elimination and the odd skeleton. If n0 is even, then C(n0) = n0/2 < n0, so
the below-start criterion (Lemma D.4) is immediate. The only nontrivial starting values are
odd. For odd n0, the full Collatz orbit alternates between odd values (where n  3n + 1)
and a deterministic chain of halvings. The odd skeleton retains only the odd-to-odd steps: the
Syracuse map T (n) = (3n + 1)/2v2(3n+1) for odd n.

Definition 9.57 (Odd-skeleton drift signal). For an odd starting value n0, let n0, n1, n2, . . . be
the Syracuse orbit (odd values only) and vt = v2(3nt + 1) the 2-adic valuation at step t. The
drift signal is

                                                      t-1        di  =  log2  3  -  vi  +  log2  1  +   1   .
                                                                                                       3ni
           xt = log2 nt - log2 n0 = di,

                                                      i=0

The orbit goes below the starting value if and only if xt < 0 for some t  1.

    The drift increment di depends on a single arithmetic quantity: the valuation vi = v2(3ni+1).
Rewriting di = -(vi - log2 3) + log2(1 + 1/(3ni)) and summing gives the centered form:

                          xj = - (vi - log2 3) +                          log2   1  +    1   .                              (50)
                                                                                        3ni

                                         i<j                         i<j

                                                                                 j

The correction j is a sum of positive terms, each bounded by 1/(3ni ln 2). For orbits that
remain above start (ni  n0 for all i < j), we have j  j/(3n0 ln 2), negligible for large n0.
Therefore crossing is equivalent to the valuation excess eventually becoming positive:

                          nj < n0                          (vi - log2 3) > j  0.                                            (51)

                                                i<j

Under the ensemble model (vi i.i.d. geometric with mean 2), the expected valuation excess per
step is 2 - log2 3  0.415, strongly positive, pushing the walk toward crossing. The below-
start question reduces to: can the correlated valuation sequence {vi} keep the cumulative excess

  (vi - log2 3) below a negligible threshold forever?

Proposition 9.58 (Odd-skeleton drift reduction [Supporting]). The Collatz conjecture for all
n0  2 is equivalent to: for every odd n0  3, the odd-skeleton drift signal satisfies xt < 0 for
some t  1.

                                                           64
Proof. If n0 is even, C(n0) < n0 immediately. If n0 is odd and xt < 0 for some t, then nt < n0
and the below-start criterion (Lemma D.4) gives convergence by induction. Conversely, if n0
converges to 1, then eventually nt = 1 < n0, so xt < 0.

Spectral properties of the drift signal. The drift increments {di} are not independent:
the Syracuse dynamics correlates consecutive valuations through the residue class nt mod 2K.

However, the correlation decays rapidly.

Observation 9.59 (Drift increment mixing and universal descent (empirical, not proved)).
The following properties are observed computationally but are not proved for general orbits.
For all 49,999 odd starting values 3  n0  99,999:

1. Every orbit descends below its start on the odd skeleton. The maximum Syracuse-step
   descent time is  = 85 at n0 = 35,655.

2. The mean drift is negative: d¯  [-2.61, -0.12] with mean -0.57.

3. The autocorrelation at lag 1 is mildly positive: (1)  0.05­0.20 (valuation clustering).

4. The autocorrelation at lag 10 is near zero: |(10)| < 0.10 for all tested orbits.

5. The sum of absolute autocorrelations  20   |(k)|  is  bounded:  mean  1.40,  max  4.43.
                                         k=1

    The summable-autocorrelation property (item 4) means the drift increments satisfy a weak-

dependence condition sufficient for the functionalCLT (Ibragimov's theorem for mixing se-
quences): the partial sums xT = i<T di obey xT / T  N (µ T , e2ff ) with effective variance
e2ff = 2(1 + 2 k1 (k)) > 0. Since µ < 0, the partial sums tend to -, and crossing occurs
with probability 1 in the ensemble.

Remark 9.60 (Why the odd skeleton is spectrally cleaner). The drift signal {di} is spectrally
cleaner than the gain observable K for three reasons:

   1. Scalar signal. di is a single real number per time step, not a function on Z/2KZ. The
       spectral analysis is classical (Fourier on Z), not Walsh on a growing group.

   2. DC encodes the density condition. The mean drift d¯ = log2 3 - v¯ is negative iff v¯ > log2 3,
       which is equivalent to the density condition  < 1/log2 3. The density model's prediction
       is directly the DC component of the drift signal.

3. Crossing is weaker than summability. The gain-budget approach requires K K < 
   (summability). The crossing approach requires only mint xt < 0 (a single negative value).

   This is a strictly weaker target.

Theorem 9.61 (Exact block law for the valuation sequence). Let aj(n) = v2(3T j(n) + 1) be

the valuation at the j-th odd-skeleton step. For any prescribed positive integers b0, . . . , bm-1,
the set of odd n with (a0(n), . . . , am-1(n)) = (b0, . . . , bm-1) is a single odd residue class modulo
2b0+···+bm-1+1. Hence its natural density among odd integers is exactly

                                              m-1

2-(b0+···+bm-1) =                                    2-bj .

                                              j=0

Proof. Induction on m. Base case (m = 1): For odd n, a0(n) = v2(3n + 1) = k iff 3n + 1  2k
(mod 2k+1), i.e. n  (2k - 1)/3 (mod 2k+1/3). Since 3 is invertible modulo every power of 2,
this defines a unique odd residue class modulo 2k+1, and 2k+1/(2 · 2k) = 1 class among the 2k
odd residues modulo 2k+1, giving density 2-k.

                                         65
    Inductive step: Suppose the block (b0, . . . , bm-1) determines a unique odd residue class
modulo M = 2b0+···+bm-1+1. The first step maps n to T (n) = (3n + 1)/2b0, which is determined
modulo M/2b0 = 2b1+···+bm-1+1. By the base case applied to T (n) in place of n with block

(b1, . . . , bm-1), the condition on the remaining m-1 valuations selects a unique odd residue class
modulo 2b1+···+bm-1+1. Pulling back through the invertible affine map n  T (n), we obtain a
unique odd residue class modulo 2b0+b1+···+bm-1+1. The density follows by counting.

Corollary 9.62 (I.i.d. valuation process). Under natural density on odd integers, the valuation
sequence (a0, a1, a2, . . .) is exactly i.i.d. with Pr(aj = k) = 2-k for k  1.

Corollary 9.63 (I.i.d. cycle types). Group the valuation sequence into run-compensate cycles
(1, . . . , 1, ri) with ri  2. Then the cycle types (L0, r0), (L1, r1), . . . are i.i.d. under natural

      Li

               Pr(Li = , ri = k) = 2-(+1) · 2-(k-1),          0, k  2.

Proof. Each cycle consumes Li + 1 valuations. By Theorem 9.61, the valuations consumed
by distinct cycles are independent (the joint density factorises). The cycle type (Li, ri) is a
deterministic function of its valuation block, so the cycle types are independent. The common
marginal law was established in Corollary 9.70.

Proposition 9.64 (Run-length invariant). For odd n  1, define the initial run length L(n) as
the number of consecutive Syracuse steps with v2(3nj + 1) = 1 before the first step with v2  2.
Then:

1. L(n) = v2(n + 1) - 1.
2. Along the run, nj + 1 = 3j(n + 1)/2j for 0  j  L(n).

3. The run terminates at nL with nL  1 (mod 4), and the net growth factor over the run
   is (3/2)L.

Proof. If v2(n + 1) = 1, then n  1 (mod 4) and v2(3n + 1)  2, so L(n) = 0. If v2(n + 1)  2,
then n  3 (mod 4) and v2(3n + 1) = 1, so n1 = (3n + 1)/2 with n1 + 1 = 3(n + 1)/2 and
v2(n1 +1) = v2(n+1)-1. Induction gives L(n) = v2(n+1)-1 and the exact formula for nj.

Corollary 9.65 (Single-cycle crossing probability). Each Syracuse orbit decomposes into run-
compensate cycles: a growth burst of L steps at rate 3/2, terminated by a compensating step
with valuation ar  2. In the ensemble (uniform odd starting point), L and ar are independent,
and the cycle produces a net descent below n0 with probability

Proof. From the run formula (Proposition 9.64), nL = 3L(n + 1)/2L - 1. Applying one Syracuse

Remark 9.67 (One-cycle crossing statistics). Computational verification over all odd n 

200,000 confirms 100% agreement with the exact criterion. Three regimes emerge: (i) always
crosses, when n(L, r) < 1, every odd n in that (L, r)-class descends in one cycle; (ii) threshold,
when n(L, r)  1, small n in the class may fail to cross; (iii) never crosses, when r < rmin(L),
no n descends.

Proposition 9.71 (Universal one-cycle crossing criterion). A single-cycle block type (L, r) forces

                              2r-1 > 2     3      L+1

                          rall(L) =  log2  2(  3  )L+1  -  1  + 2.
                                               2

Proof. From Theorem 9.66, n(L, r) = (3L+1 - 2L+1)/(2L+r - 3L+1). The condition n < 1

Proposition 9.73 (Universal crossing criterion for finite cycle blocks). Let  = (L0, r0), . . . ,

                       k-1    B0 = 0, Bj+1 = (Lj, rj) Bj + (Lj, rj).

             k = (Lj, rj),

                       j=0

                          k < 1 and Bk < 1 - k.                           (52)

Remark 9.80 (Interpretation and comparison). Proposition 9.79 implies that under the i.i.d.
ensemble, the probability that no prefix of the first k cycles is universally crossing decays
exponentially. For almost every odd starting value, a finite prefix of cycles forces every member
of its residue class below its start, not merely the random starting point itself, but all odd
integers sharing its 2-adic residue class. This is strictly stronger than the almost-all crossing
theorem (Theorem 9.154), which guarantees crossing only for the specific starting value. The
result is derived through a completely different mechanism (Kesten random affine recursion)
from Tao's [14] almost-all theorem (entropy methods), yet arrives at a compatible and sharper
conclusion.

Remark 9.81 (Pointwise verification of universal crossing). Every odd n0 with 3  n0  10,001
was verified to possess a universal crossing prefix: a k-block satisfying (52) where k ranges
from 1 (the majority) to 27 (worst case n0 = 6375, whose orbit begins with two non-crossing
cycles (L = 2, r = 2) followed by a spike at cycle 10 with  = 43.25). The family 2m - 1 for
m = 1, . . . , 39 has universal prefixes at k  40 (worst: 233 - 1 at k = 40). The notorious n0 = 27
maintains k > 1 through k = 14 (the orbit climbs to 9,232 before descending), yet achieves a
universal prefix at k = 16 with n = 0.66.

    If the pointwise conclusion of Proposition 9.79, that every orbit eventually hits a universal
prefix, could be proved for all n0, the Collatz conjecture would follow immediately: strong
induction applies because universal crossing forces every odd integer in the class below its
start. The distributional-to-pointwise barrier is thus equivalent to proving that no orbit can
systematically avoid universal block types forever.

Remark 9.82 (Adversarial block types: why Route A fails). One might hope to prove Pcum(k) 
1 by showing that every k-block eventually satisfies the universal crossing criterion (52). A
beam-search optimization reveals that this hope is false: for each k, there exist crossing k-
blocks whose threshold value n = Bk/(1 - k) grows exponentially with k.

    The adversary's strategy is to use (k - m) non-crossing cycles (e.g., (L, r) = (5, 2) with
 = 729/128  5.70) to inflate Bk exponentially, then exactly m strongly crossing cycles (e.g.,
(0, 3) with  = 3/8) to bring k just below 1. Since the minimum number of corrective cycles
m  k · ln nc/(ln nc - ln c) grows linearly in k, the inflated numerator Bk outpaces the
denominator (1 - k), yielding max n  exp(0.023 k).

    Crucially, these adversarial blocks have vanishing probability: a block using a copies of (5, 2)
has probability  (1/128)a, giving log10 Pr  -2.1a. At k = 25, the worst block has probability
< 10-33. Thus Pcum(k)  1 holds (Proposition 9.79), not because every block crosses, but
because the non-crossing blocks have measure shrinking faster than their n values grow.

    However, these adversarial blocks are uniformly fragile: Proposition 9.84 below shows that
for every a  1, adding a single (0, 3) cycle beyond the first negative-drift prefix collapses n
below 1. The adversary must stop at exactly the tuned length; one more crossing cycle destroys
the construction. A real orbit, whose cycle types are determined by 2-adic arithmetic rather
than adversarial choice, has no mechanism to maintain this tuning.

    This closes Route A: the pointwise conjecture cannot be reduced to a universal combinatorial
statement about block types, but the adversarial counterexamples are structurally unstable
(Propositions 9.84 and 9.85).

Proposition 9.85 (General pair-level fragility criterion). Let A = (LA, rA) be any expanding

cycle type (A > 1) and B = (LB, rB) any contracting type (0 < B < 1). Define A :=

                                      B + B(1 + A) < 1                                        (56)
