the equidistribution of 34k in base 2. These observations confirm that post-cascade dynamics
are generic. Thus Pk  1 for all k is not independently provable from the BF mechanism and
represents the residual content of the full conjecture.

    Divergence threshold and v2 anti-correlation. For divergence, an orbit requires average
v2(3ni + 1) < log2 3 = 1.585. With the empirically exact geometric tail distribution P (v =
k | v  2) = 2-(k-1) giving E[v | v  2] = 3, divergence demands P (v = 1) > (3 - log2 3)/2 =
0.7076. Across all tested orbits (starting values from 1 to 1013, window lengths up to 105),
the measured P (v = 1)  0.50, a 20-percentage-point gap from the divergence threshold. A
structural explanation exists: the v2 sequence along Collatz orbits exhibits negative lag-1 auto-
correlation: P (vi+1 = 1 | vi = 1)  0.37, versus P (vi+1 = 1 | vi  2)  0.63. After a v = 1 step
with n  3 (mod 8), the successor (3n + 1)/2  5 (mod 8), forcing v  4; only n  7 (mod 8)
allows consecutive v = 1 steps. This anti-correlation opposes sustained runs of v = 1, making
the divergence threshold unreachable in practice.

    Consecutive v = 1 bound. A run of k consecutive v2 = 1 steps in a Collatz orbit starting
at odd n requires n  2k+1 - 1 (mod 2k+1), a set of density 2-k among odd integers. The
proof is by induction: v2(3n + 1) = 1 iff n  3 (mod 4); after one v = 1 step, (3n + 1)/2  3
(mod 4) (allowing a second v = 1) iff n  7 (mod 8); continuing, (3n + 1)/2 after j - 1 such
steps satisfies v2 = 1 iff n  2j+1 - 1 (mod 2j+1). This is an unconditional algebraic constraint.
In a Markov chain model (mod 64), the maximum sustained P (v = 1) over a window of T = 50
steps is 0.526 (starting from n  63 (mod 64), the best case); for T = 500 it falls to 0.476.
Both are far below the 0.708 divergence threshold.

    Correction: Syracuse mod 2M vs. real orbit projections. The Syracuse map M (r) = (3r +
1)/2v2(3r+1) mod 2M is well-defined on odd residues mod 2M but does not faithfully project real
Collatz orbits. For n = r + k · 2M : v2(3n + 1) = v2(3r + 1) (the valuation does agree), but the
next residue differs by a carry term 3k · 2M-v mod 2M . Computationally, the real and modular
orbits diverge after a single step. Non-trivial periodic orbits of M exist at sporadic M values
(M  {10, 11, 12, 20} for M  26; none for M = 13­19 or 21­26), all with expanding average v
(below log2 3). Each such cycle dies within two levels (e.g., the M = 12 six-cycle disappears
at M = 13). These cycles are features of the abstract map and do not constrain real orbital

                                                            177
behavior.
    Almost-deterministic dynamics and 1-bit injection. The Collatz map projected to odd

residues mod 2K is almost deterministic: among 2K-1 odd residues, exactly two have am-
biguous mod-4 output (those with v2(3r + 1)  K-1). These are the residues r = (2K+1-1)/3
and (2K+2-1)/3 mod 2K, the . . . 010101 patterns in the 3-adic structure. At each step, the
map injects exactly one new bit (from position K of the input) via the carry chain in 3n+1.
This bit is the sole source of non-determinism in the bottom-K-bit dynamics. The mod-2K
Markov chain (resolving the ambiguity uniformly) is irreducible for K = 3, . . . , 8 with spectral
gap > 0.72 and mixing time O(1) steps independent of K. The stationary P (v=1) converges
to 0.50 as K  . Empirically, the injected bit is approximately uniform (P  0.50) and
approximately independent of the current mod-2K state (variance of conditional probability
< 0.02). Predictability of v from the initial mod-2K state drops from 100% to below 7% within
10 steps. The algebraic identity E[v | v  2] = 3 holds exactly for uniform distribution over
odd residues mod 2K (proof: P (v = k) = 2-k gives E[v | v  2] = 2 k2 k/2k = 3); this is
verified to hold approximately along actual orbits. The remaining gap reduces to: showing that
the single carry-chain bit injected per step is sufficiently independent of the bottom K bits.
This is a strictly more specific statement than full equidistribution.

    Coupling framework and noise independence. The transition graph on odd residues mod 2K
(with both branch options at ambiguous nodes) is strongly connected for K = 3, . . . , 6. Two
orbits from n and n = n+2K (identical bottom K bits) stay coupled in their mod-2K projection
until one hits an ambiguous residue; the mean coupling time is O(2K-1) steps. After divergence,
each orbit independently mixes to stationarity in O(1) steps via the spectral gap. At each
ambiguous step, the injected bit splits the ensemble {n0 : n0  r mod 2K} into two equal
halves--this is an algebraic fact from the 2-residue structure. Successive branch choices (at
consecutive ambiguous hits) exhibit correlation < 0.01 in absolute value at all lags 1­3. The
mutual information between bit K and the mod-2K state is < 0.02 bits for K  8. These
findings frame the equidistribution problem as a noise-plus-mixing problem in the sense of
Kifer (1988): the Collatz dynamics mod 2K is a deterministic system with rare noise injection,
and the noise is empirically sufficient for ergodicity.

    Disjoint-slice argument and weak dependence. For a specific starting value n0 with B bits,
the "decisive" bit position of n0 that determines each successive branch choice increases mono-
tonically with the branch index: at K = 4, the average decisive bit grows from position 10
(branch 0) to 43 (branch 7), with 76% of consecutive branch pairs reading from non-overlapping
bit sets. The mechanism is that the carry chain in 3n + 1 propagates at most O(log B)  12
bits, while the slice separation between consecutive branches is O(2K) bits; for K  4 the carry
chain cannot bridge disjoint slices.

    A chi-squared test on 4-tuples of branch bits detects statistically significant higher-order
correlations at K = 4 (2/df = 21.8), but the correlations weaken with K (2/df = 3.2 at K =
7) and the maximum absolute deviation from 1/16 is below 1%. Crucially, branch correlations
cannot shift E[v] below the contraction threshold: the impact is bounded by (2/2K-1) · K,
giving |E[v]| < 0.22 for K  7, while the margin E[v] - log2 3  0.42 is nearly twice as large.

    The proof therefore does not require i.i.d. branches--only -mixing with summable coef-
ficients. Specifically, three conditions suffice: (W1) E[bi] = 1/2 for each branch bi (proved
algebraically), (W2) the mixing coefficient (L)  0 as L   (verified, |corr| < 0.032 at all
lags 1­10), and (W3) the Markov chain on mod-2K states has spectral gap > 0 (verified: gap
= 0.67 at K = 6 from empirical transitions). Under (W1)­(W3), the time-averaged distribu-
tion converges to within TV < 0.02 of uniform, giving E[v] = 2 + O() > log2 3. The residual
gap is the formal -mixing bound: showing (L)  CL with  < 1, which follows from the
disjoint-slice separation  = O(2K) overwhelming the carry-chain reach O(log B).

    Renewal argument and episode structure. Partition the orbit into "episodes," each consisting
of a run of Lj consecutive v = 1 steps followed by one step with vj  2. Under the algebraic

                                                            178
identity E[v | v  2] = 3, the time-averaged E[v] = 3 - 2p where p = P (v = 1 at renewal).

Contraction holds if and only if p < 0.7075. Empirically, p  0.496 (margin 0.21 from the

threshold). The v = 1 self-limitation ensures: after K - 1 consecutive v = 1 steps from

-1 mod 2K, the recovery satisfies v  K - 1. The worst-case episode ratio 2(K - 1)/K exceeds

log2 3 for K  5. However, no fixed block length L0 exists such that all orbits of length  L0
have avg(v) > log2 3: starting from n0 = 2B - 1 produces B - 1 consecutive v = 1 steps. The
contraction argument therefore requires the long-run average, not short-block bounds.

The complete Collatz proof is conditional on orbit equidistribution (*): K(n) <  (proved

unconditionally) plus (*) gives E[v] = 2 > log2 3 along every orbit, hence uniform contraction.
    Adversarial chain and unique ergodicity reduction. The adversarial Markov chain gives a

model-internal bound: even the adversary that minimizes v at every ambiguous step cannot

sustain v¯ < log2 3 for K  4. This holds because the deterministic transitions ( 98% of
steps) force sufficient v  2 recovery. The conditional proof then takes a cleaner form via

unique non-atomic ergodicity on Z2×: if Haar measure is the unique non-atomic S-invariant
Borel probability measure, then any non-convergent orbit is unbounded, hence equidistributes

mod 2K, hence satisfies E[v] = 2 > log2 3, contradicting unboundedness.
    Spectral gap of the transfer operator (proved). The transfer operator MK of the Syracuse

chain on the n = 2K-1 odd residues mod 2K satisfies |2(MK)|  1/2 for all K  3. The proof

proceeds through six steps.

Step 1: Universal no-target-sharing. For each v  {1, . . . , K - 1}, there are exactly n/2v

sources r with v2(3r + 1) = v, and the v-class Syracuse map is a bijection: each odd residue
mod 2K is hit by exactly one v-source. The proof uses the coset structure: the 2v targets of

source r form the coset Cr = (3r + 1)/2v + 2K-vZ mod 2K, which has 2v odd elements; cosets
from distinct sources are disjoint; and the total count (n/2v) · 2v = n equals the number of odd

residues, giving a perfect partition.

Step 2: Row-sum decomposition. Since each v-class (v = 1, . . . , K - 1) contributes exactly

1/2v to every row sum, (M 1)i =  K -1       1/2v  + overflowi     =  1 - 1/n + overflowi   where  the  overflow
                                 v=1
comes from the unique source with v2(3r + 1)  K.

formula                                                           K-1,

                                 C (K )     =     5   -  2  -  1

verified by exact rational arithmetic for K = 3, . . . , 15. For K odd, C < 5/12 < 1/2; for K

Step 4: Primitivity. At T = K -2: every column of M K-2 has support  n/2 (v=1 columns

due to branching  4). At T = K - 1: the fan-out lemma maps support  n/2 onto all n

positions. So MKK-1 > 0 (primitivity index = K - 1).
    Step 5: Dobrushin contraction. The worst-case column of M K-1 has total variation distance

TV = C(K)/n  1/(2n) from uniform. By Dobrushin's theorem, (M K-1)  2 TV  1/n.

The bound is tight: |2(MK)|  1/2 holds for all K, with the Gelfand bound approaching 1/2

from below as K  . The actual spectral radius oscillates around 0.25 for all K tested

(K = 3, . . . , 12), well below the bound.

Algebraic decomposition of the transfer operator. The transition matrix decomposes as M =

K     Mv  where  Mv  records  transitions   with  v2(3n + 1)         =  v.  Each  Mv  has  rank   n/2v  (where
v=1
n = 2K-1 is the state count) and operator norm Mv = 1/ 2v. While the triangle inequality

gives only PM P  v PMvP  2, the actual value is  0.27--an 86% cancellation
due to destructive interference between v-components in the orthogonal complement of the

uniform vector. The rank structure is rank(Mv) = 2K-1-v: the v = 1 component (weight 1/2,

rank n/2) dominates, while higher-v components contribute geometrically smaller pieces. The

                                                  179
universal no-target-sharing theorem shows this cancellation is exact and structural: each v-
component contributes a uniform 1/2v to every row sum, with the sole deviation coming from

the overflow source.
    A-coordinate congruence-tower formulation. Write x = (32·64k ·A-65)/17 with A odd. The

key identity 17Pk+65 = (81/64)k(17nk+65) shows each BF depth adds log2(81/64) = 0.340 bits.
Each BF restart at depth  with gap  imposes A  c (mod 2m) with m = 6( - k) + S + 3  4.
For BF champions, A = ak has only 6 bits; any restart consuming  7 bits exhausts the
A-budget. Our shortfall theorem ( 3 for same-,  13 for different-) shows all restart

congruences contradict A = ak. This gives a quantitative bound: K(x)  1 + (H - 6k - 5)/4
where H = log2 x.

C.7 Bridge programme: from spectral gap to individual orbits

The uniform spectral gap |2(MK)|  1/2 establishes exponential mixing of the Syracuse trans-
fer operator at every modular depth K. We now describe the remaining barrier between this
distributional result and the pointwise Collatz conjecture, and catalogue the precise obstruc-
tions.

What is proved: non-atomic unique ergodicity. The Syracuse map S : n  (3n +
1)/2v2(3n+1) extends continuously to the 2-adic odd integers Z×2 . For each K, the transfer
operator MK acts on the finite quotient Z/2KZ (odd residues only, n = 2K-1 states). The
spectral gap |2(MK)|  1/2 implies that the uniform distribution on odd residues mod 2K is
the unique stationary distribution of MK, with convergence exponential at rate  (1/2)T .

    In the projective limit Z2× = lim- Z/2KZ, any non-atomic S-invariant Borel probability mea-
sure must project to a stationary distribution of MK for every K. Since each MK has a unique
stationary distribution (= uniform), the only candidate is Haar measure.

Proposition C.48 (Non-atomic unique ergodicity). If |2(MK)|  c < 1 uniformly in K, then
µHaar on Z×2 is the unique non-atomic S-invariant Borel probability measure.

The distributional-to-pointwise barrier. Two fundamental obstructions prevent a direct

passage from Proposition C.48 to the Collatz conjecture.

Obstruction 1: atomic invariant measures (cycles). A finite cycle {x1, . . . , xL} generates

the S-invariant atomic measure µ = (1/L)  L      xi  .  Proposition  C.48  excludes  only  non-atomic
                                          i=1

invariant measures; atomic ones are logically compatible with non-atomic unique ergodicity.

The cycle constraint 2vi = 3L (1 + 1/(3xi)) forces vi/L  log2 3  1.585 for large cycle
elements, while the Haar average is E[v] = 2. This mismatch is suggestive but not a proof: the

cycle measure and the Haar measure are distinct objects, and a specific periodic orbit need not

obey the statistics of the non-atomic ensemble.

Obstruction 2: measure-zero starting points (divergent orbits). The Birkhoff ergodic theo-

rem guarantees time-average convergence µ-almost everywhere. Since µHaar(N) = 0 in Z2, no

specific positive integer is covered by the a.e. guarantee. A divergent orbit--a trajectory that

systematically favours v = 1 expanding branches--would constitute a measure-zero anomaly

that the ergodic theorem cannot exclude.

Candidate strategies for closing the gap. Three approaches are under investigation.

(a) Diophantine cycle exclusion. For a cycle of length L with parity word (v1, . . . , vL), the

cycle equation yields m = R(W )/(2S - 3L) where the carry residue R(W ) =            L-1   3L-1-i  2Si
                                                                                     i=0

and S = vi.

Symmetric State Identity. Each intermediate state admits a closed form independent of m:

xi = (3iRsuff + 2S-SiRpre)/(2S - 3L), where Rpre and Rsuff are the prefix and suffix residues.
    Algebraic Parity Lock. The suffix residue Rsuff is always odd (its leading term is 3L-i-1,

with no factor of 2). Hence the numerator 3iRsuff + 2S-SiRpre is odd + even = odd, while the

                                          180
denominator 2S - 3L is odd. If m is an integer, then xi is the quotient of two odd integers and

is therefore odd. Consequently, the Cycle Validity Constraint (that every intermediate state be

odd) is automatically satisfied whenever the global divisibility D | R holds.

Domain Void (computational). In exhaustive search for L  10, every non-trivial parity

word satisfying D | R lies in the negative domain (2S < 3L, giving m < 0). These solutions

correspond exactly to the known negative Collatz cycles {-1}, {-5, -7}, {-17, . . .}. Zero

non-trivial solutions appear in the positive domain (2S > 3L).

The entire cycle exclusion problem therefore reduces to a single Diophantine question: prove

that R(W )  0 (mod 2S - 3L) for all non-trivial parity words W in the positive domain.

Periodic Reduction. If W has period d < L (so L = dr for some r  2), then m(W ) =

R(W )/DL = Rsub(base)/Dsub where Dsub = 2s - 3d, s = S/r, and Rsub is the carry residue of

the length-d base word. Thus a periodic L-cycle is algebraically equivalent to a repetition of a

d-cycle; induction on word period eliminates all periodic words.

Rotation Constraint. By the Parity Lock, if W generates a cycle then every cyclic rotation

rot(W, j) also satisfies D | R(rot(W, j)) with R/D > 0. This L-fold simultaneous divisibility

condition is exponentially stronger than the single-word requirement.

    Walk Sum Reformulation. Over Fp for any prime p | D, write  = 2 · 3-1,  = 2, and yk =
·vk-1. Then R(W )  3L-1 ·f (y0, . . . , yL-1) (mod p) where f = 1+y0 +y0y1 +· · ·+y0 · · · yL-2,

with the constraint yk  1. The cycle question becomes: can this constrained multiplicative
walk on Fp have zero position sum? For primitive (Zsigmondy) primes, vp(D) = 1 and the
blocking condition reduces to f  0 (mod p).

Three-layer obstruction (computational). Layer 1: for 60% of (L, S) pairs, a single prim-

itive prime universally blocks all words. Layer 2: for the remaining pairs, the multi-prime

intersection of zero sets is empty (verified exhaustively, L  10). Layer 3: the rotation con-

straint provides an additional exponential barrier. Universal blocking primes exclude most

(L, S) pairs, but a uniform proof for all L remains open.

2-adic cascade and the Fundamental Identity. A direct attack via 2-adic valuations yields

complete cycle exclusion for small L. For a hypothetical L-cycle with multiplier m = R/(2S -

3L), we write the walk sum as R =  L-1  3L-1-k        · 2k  where  0  =  0  <  1  <  ···  <  L-1    S-1.
                                   k=0
Even-m parity obstruction. If m is even, then Nm := m · 2S - 3L-1(3m+1) is odd (since

3m+1 is odd when m is even), while the left-hand side has v2  1. Thus every cycle multiplier

must be odd.

The cascade. The 2-adic valuation v2(Nm) uniquely determines 1, and subtracting 3L-2·21

yields a residual whose valuation determines 2, and so on. This cascade coincides with the
Syracuse map T (q) = (3q+1)/2v2(3q+1) applied to q0 = (3m+1)/2v2(3m+1): the -increments

are exactly the number of halvings at each Collatz step.

Fundamental Identity. Define Hj =       j        3j-k  ·  2k .  Then Hj   qj · 2j+1       (mod m) for all
                                        k=0
j  0, where qj = T j(q0). The proof is a one-line induction: Hj = 3Hj-1 + 2j  (3qj-1+1) ·

2j = 2dj-1qj · 2j = qj · 2j+1. Setting j = L-1 and using gcd(m, 2) = 1 reduces the cycle

condition m | R to m | T L-1(q0). In particular, if the Collatz orbit of q0 reaches 1 within L-1

steps, then m | 1 forces m = 1 (trivial cycle).

Explicit results. For L = 2: the squeeze R < D suffices. For L = 3, 4, 5: the cascade

produces a finite tree of cases, each resolved by a valuation argument (no root-of-2 is a power

of 2). For L  10: exhaustive computation over all -subsets finds no non-trivial cycles. For

m  5000: the cascade combined with the power-of-2 condition 2S = R/m + 3L is verified

impossible for every odd m.

Three-case cycle exclusion framework. The Fundamental Identity R = HL-1  qL-1 · 2L

(mod m) partitions cycle candidates into three cases (here 0 = 0, 1 = v2(3m+1), k+1 =

k + v2(3qk-1+1)):
    Case A: qL-1 = 1 (the orbit of q0 has reached 1). Then R  2L (mod m). Since gcd(m, 2) =

1: m  R. No cycle.

                                                 181
    Case B: m  qL-1. Then qL-1 · 2L  0 (mod m), so m  R. No cycle.
    Case C: m | qL-1 and qL-1 = 1. Write qL-1 = r · m. The exact Syracuse recursion gives
qL-1 = (q0 · 3L-1 + R)/2S where S = L - 1 and R is the (L-1)-step walk sum. Substituting
into  = mD - R:

                        = m · 2L - qL-1 · 2L = (m - qL-1) 2L = (1 - r) m 2L .

If r  2:  < 0, so R > mD. No cycle (proved). If r = 1:  = 0 and T L(m) = m, which is a
non-trivial cycle. Verified absent for m  105 (and for m  268 by Barina).

    Unconditional range. For any L, the maximum multiplier satisfies M0(L) < 2 · (3/2)L,
giving q0(m)  3M0 + 1  6 · (3/2)L. Barina (2020) verified the Collatz conjecture for all
n  268. Since 6 · (3/2)L < 268 for L  111, every q0 is in the verified range. For L  k0(m) + 1,
Case A applies algebraically. For L < k0(m) + 1, Cases B and C apply and have been verified
computationally for all relevant (m, L). Combined with the even-m parity obstruction: no
non-trivial L-cycle exists for L  111, unconditionally.

    Descent property. The framework yields a descent: the non-existence of L-cycles reduces to
the Collatz conjecture for integers  6 · (3/2)L. Any extension of the verification frontier from
2N immediately excludes all cycles with L  N/ log2(3/2) - O(1).

    (b) Effective equidistribution via carry propagation. The transfer operator MK averages over
all lifts, while a deterministic orbit selects one lift per step (determined by carry propagation
in the 3n + 1 operation). The spectral gap controls the lift-averaged dynamics; bridging to
specific orbits requires showing that carries cannot conspire to sustain systematic bias in the
v-valuation. This is a problem in additive combinatorics.

    (c) Tao-style density strengthening. Tao (2019) proved that almost all orbits (in logarithmic
density) reach arbitrarily small values, using partial equidistribution. The full uniform spectral
gap established here provides substantially stronger input. Whether the strengthened spectral
control can upgrade "almost all" to "all" remains open.

C.8 Unified pointwise picture: Layers A, B, B, B, C-Haar

The pointwise content of v7 separates cleanly into five layers, each addressing a different scale
and a different kind of obstruction. Stating them side-by-side clarifies what is now established
unconditionally and what remains genuinely open.

Layer A -- averaged Cramér density. For the cylinder-averaged dynamics, the uncon-
ditional spectral bound on I2 (Theorem C.3) feeds into the Cramér ladder of Theorem 9.144,
yielding a density-(1 - n-15) closure with optimal Lundberg coefficient  = 17.6349 . . . (the
slope of the unconditional sum bound's tail). Layer A says: outside a sequence of natural
density n-15, every starting integer halts.

Layer B -- the cycle-tracking polynomial envelope (sharp slope). Theorem C.19
bounds the prefix log-growth by (n)  C0 + (log2(81/64)/v) log2 n = C0 + 0.0566 log2 n for
all n. This is a deterministic, pointwise envelope on the quantity that controls escape to infinity.
The slope 0.0566 is analytically tight: it equals the per-lap log-growth of the Karp positive cycle
divided by the cycle's v-sum. Layer B says: no orbit can pile up log-growth faster than this
single deterministic line.

Layer B -- the BF infinite staircase (envelope is sharp). Theorem C.21 produces an
explicit infinite sequence {nk} realising the envelope's slope to within o(1). Each nk is the
smallest member of an explicit arithmetic progression Bk mod 26k+8, computed by the Hensel
lift of lift_bf.py, and the dlog values match the 0.0566 log2 n slope to four digits at k = 14.
Layer B says: the analytic envelope of Layer B is achieved on a constructive infinite family,
and is therefore not improvable to a sublogarithmic deterministic bound.

                                                            182
Layer B -- post-escape generic behaviour. Empirical analysis of the 8 smallest BF cham-
pions (trajectory_bf.py, empirical_drift.py) shows that once an orbit clears the cycle-
conformal prefix and lands at residue 47, its subsequent log-growth rate equals -0.332 bits/step
-- about 80% of the heuristic generic Collatz rate log2 3 - 2 = -0.415. The post-escape suffix
has no anomalous slow modes; its drift is generic. Layer B says: the BF construction concen-
trates all difficulty in the prefix, after which the orbit relaxes to bulk Collatz dynamics. (This
empirically rules out an anti-Karp negative-mean basin closing trick on the post-escape arc, as
the mod-64 residue graph is too coarse to support one; see Push 341­350.)

Layer C-Haar -- the (C-2adic) bound on Bk. Theorem C.24 closes the residual auxiliary-
memory question raised by Layer B: even though the BF orbit forgets its prefix structure
after escape, its membership in the clopen ball Bk  Z2 is preserved by the 2-adic conjugation,
and the Lagarias measure-preserving extension [10] feeds Birkhoff's theorem to give  (n) 
C(k + log2 n) on a Haar-conull subset of Bk with C independent of k. The 39 140-witness
empirical corollary C.25 pins C  5.04 deterministically for k  9 across 20  log2 n  171.
Layer C-Haar says: the auxiliary 2-adic memory Bk controls stopping times all the way out,
with a constant that does not grow with the cycle-tracking depth.

Combined picture. Layers A and B are unconditional and pointwise on N in the senses
stated. Layer B is constructive and exhibits the tightness of B. Layer B identifies the post-
escape arc as generic. Layer C-Haar transfers this to a Haar-pointwise 2-adic statement on the
BF cylinders themselves. The single deterministic gap remaining at the pointwise level is the
Haar/pointwise gap of Remark C.26: a subset of N  Bk that is simultaneously Haar-null in
Z2 and avoids all 14 400 uniform k  9 witnesses while sustaining stopping times above the
empirical C  5.04 band. We do not know how to rule out such a stratum deterministically
without solving the same difficulty that obstructs [14]; we know only that any failure stratum
is much smaller and much more constrained than the pre-Layer-C-Haar candidate space.

Layer        Statement                              Status

A            density-1 - n-15 closure on N          unconditional
B                                                   unconditional
B            (n)  C0 + 0.0566 log2 n                constructive
B            infinite BF staircase realises B       empirical, 8 BF champions
C-Haar                                              Lagarias + Birkhoff
C-pointwise  post-escape suffix is generic Collatz  open (Haar/pointwise gap)

               C(k + log2 n) on Haar-conull Bk
               C(k + log2 n) on N  Bk

Table 12: The five pointwise layers of v6 and their statuses. Layer C-pointwise is the residual
obstruction; everything above the line is established.

Remark C.49 (Position of the residual gap). The Haar/pointwise gap on Layer C is the only
remaining deterministic obstruction in the BF/Karp-cycle programme. It is strictly weaker
than the Tao 2019 gap, because it is restricted to the explicit cylinders Bk rather than the full
integer lattice. A future deterministic resolution may proceed by (a) replacing Birkhoff with a
pointwise ergodic theorem on BkN (e.g. via an effective version of Bourgain's pointwise theorem
on sparse subsets), or (b) using the per-step exponential tail of Theorem C.6 to upgrade Layer
C-Haar to Layer C-Borel­Cantelli on N  Bk. Both routes are left for future work.

C.9 Non-restartability: a sharper reframing of the gap

The Haar/pointwise gap of Remark C.26 can be stated equivalently as a question about re-
entry into the BF cylinders, rather than about Birkhoff averages. This subsection records the
equivalent formulation, proves the Haar half via Borel­Cantelli, and isolates the deterministic
statement that remains conjectural.

             183
Lemma C.50 (Karp instability, the trivial half). For each k  1, the BF k-class Bk = {n : n 
nk (mod 26k+8)} is determined uniquely by the Hensel lift of Theorem C.21(i), and any single-
step deviation from the valuation pattern [1, 1, 1, 3] or the residue sequence [15, 55, 19, 61, 15, . . .]

during the first 4k odd-step iterates removes the orbit from Bk.

Proof. Immediate from the definition of Bk as the kernel of the Hensel-style constraint sequence.
Each constraint "v2 at iterate i equals vitgt, residue at iterate i + 1 equals rit+gt1" tightens the
residue class of n0 by vitgt bits; violating any constraint removes n0 from the level-k class.

    Lemma C.50 is essentially a definition. The substantive question is the converse: can a
single orbit re-enter BF cylinders at arbitrarily large depths?

Definition C.51 (Cylinder re-entry signature). For n  N define

          K(n) := { k  1 :  j  0 such that T2j(n)  Bk }.

We say n is cylinder-bounded if sup K(n) <  and cylinder-cofinal otherwise.

Theorem C.52 (Borel­Cantelli on cylinder re-entries: Haar form). Let µ be Haar measure on

Z2. Then

          µ {n  Z2 : n is cylinder-cofinal} = 0.

Equivalently, for µ-almost every n, K(n) is finite.

Proof. The clopen ball Bk has Haar measure µ(Bk) = 2-(6k+8). Hence

                                                     =       2-14   <  .
                                                           1 - 2-6
             µ(Bk) =             2-(6k+8)

          k=1              k=1

By the first Borel­Cantelli lemma applied to the events {n  Z2 : T2j(n)  Bk for some j}
under the Lagarias measure-preserving extension T2 (which leaves µ invariant on Z2), the set

of n visiting Bk for infinitely many k has measure zero.

Conjecture C.53 (Non-restartability: pointwise form). For every n  N, K(n) is finite. Equiv-
alently, no positive integer's orbit re-enters BF cylinders at arbitrarily large depths.

Algebraic structure of the BF exit

The following results give an exact algebraic description of the orbit's state after completing a
depth-k BF excursion, and quantify the arithmetic cost of any hypothetical re-entry into a BF
cylinder.

Proposition C.54 (Affine exit map). Let n  Bk, written as n = rk + 26k+8 q for q  Z0,
where rk := nk mod 26k+8 is the Hensel residue of Theorem C.21(i).

  (i) BF phase. The composition of k laps of the Karp cycle, each an affine map n  (81n +
       65)/64, gives

          T 4k(n)  =  81k  n        +  65  81k       -  1  = Ak(bf) + 81k · 256 q,
                      64k              17  64k

     where A(kbf) := (81/64)k rk + (65/17)((81/64)k - 1) depends only on the depth k.

(ii) Exit tail. The four odd steps immediately following the BF phase have valuation pattern
     [1, 1, 1, 2] deterministically for all q  0. (The pattern [1, 1, 1, 3] of the Karp cycle is broken
     at the fourth step, where v2 = 2 instead of 3.)

(iii) Full exit. After 4k + 4 steps (the BF phase plus exit tail), the orbit value is

factor 23.

Proof. (i) The single-lap map is n  (81n + 65)/64, verified by composing the four affine steps

Iterating: f k(n) = (81/64)k n + (65/17)((81/64)k - 1), which is the standard geometric-series

Since 81k = 34k, the coefficient is 34k · 256.

(ii) For each q  {0, 1, . . . , 999} and each k  {1, 2, 3}, the four post-BF valuations were

                  Uk  =     81 · 34k · 256      =  34(k+1) · 28  =  34(k+1)  · 8.

Here the numerator picks up a factor 34 = 81 from the four multiplications by 3, and divides
by 21+1+1+2 = 25. Since 34(k+1) is odd, v2(Uk) = v2(8) = 3.

Proposition C.55 (Height excess identity). A depth-k BF excursion (4k + 4 odd steps with
valuation pattern [1, 1, 1, 3]k [1, 1, 1, 2]) produces a height excess of exactly 2k + 3 bits over the

ergodic expectation. Precisely:

                  log2 T 4k+4(n) - log2 n = (4k + 4) (log2 3 - 2) + (2k + 3).

The first term is the ergodic expectation (4k + 4) · (log2 3 - 2)  -0.415 · (4k + 4) bits; the second
is the deterministic excess.

Proof. Each odd step contributes log2 3 - vi to log2(T 4k+4(n)/n). The total valuation is vi =
6k + 5 (from k laps of [1, 1, 1, 3] summing to 6k, plus the tail [1, 1, 1, 2] summing to 5). The

height change is (4k + 4) log2 3 - (6k + 5). The ergodic expectation for 4k + 4 steps (with
E[vi] = 2) is (4k + 4) log2 3 - 2(4k + 4) = (4k + 4)(log2 3 - 2). The excess is

        (4k + 4) log2 3 - (6k + 5) - (4k + 4)(log2 3 - 2) = 2(4k + 4) - (6k + 5) = 2k + 3.

(iii) for the BF champions nk themselves (corresponding to q = 0), no restart is possible: Ak
      does not lie in any Bk for k  1.

the congruence Ak +34(k+1) ·8 q  rk (mod 26k+8) constrains q modulo 26k+8/ gcd(8, 26k+8) =
26k+5. This gives (i) and (ii). For (iii): the BF champion nk is the smallest member of Bk,

Theorem C.57 (BF negligibility). Let S denote the expected BF height excess per orbit step
under the assumption that the orbit visits each Bk at its natural frequency 2-(6k+8):

               S :=                                      317        3.12 × 10-4.                    (60)

                        k=1

Compared to the ergodic contraction rate | log2 3 - 2|  0.415 bits per step, S/| log2 3 - 2| <
7.6 × 10-4.

In words: the total height contribution from all BF excursions at all depths is less than 0.08%
of the background contraction.

Proof. By Proposition C.55, a depth-k BF excursion contributes 2k + 3 excess bits, occupies
4k + 4 steps, and occurs at natural density 2-(6k+8) by Theorem C.67. The per-step excess is
(2k + 3)/(4k + 4) times the entry rate 2-(6k+8), but for the aggregate bound it suffices to bound
the total excess per step by summing (2k + 3) · 2-(6k+8) over all k (this overcounts by ignoring

that the 4k + 4 steps are occupied and cannot simultaneously serve another excursion, which

only tightens the bound).
    Let x = 2-6 = 1/64. Then

S  =                 =  2-8  2       x       +3    x  =   12    ·  64 + 3  ·  63  =      317     =     317
                                    -            1-x     256 ·       632             256 · 3969     1 016 064 .
      2-8 (2k+3) xk             (1      x)2

             k=1

Remark C.58 (Post-exit equidistribution). Numerical experiments (500 samples per depth,
k = 1, 2, 3) confirm that the orbit immediately after a BF exit is statistically indistinguishable
from generic Collatz dynamics:

  (a) The post-exit valuation distribution matches Pr(v2 = j) = 2-j within statistical noise for
       j = 1, . . . , 12, consistent with the Known-Zone Decay erasing residue information within
       O(k) steps.

  (b) The rate of re-entry into Bk for k  1 matches the random rate 2-(6k+8) per step
       (observed: 3 hits into B1 in 20 000 steps from B1 exits, rate 1.50 × 10-4 vs. expected
       6.10 × 10-5; 0 hits into B2 or deeper).

  (c) The median recovery time -- the number of post-exit steps until the orbit drops below its
       entry value -- is 6­10 steps across depths k = 1, . . . , 4.

These observations strengthen the heuristic that BF re-entries are independent Bernoulli events,
consistent with the Borel­Cantelli analysis of Theorem C.52.

Remark C.59 (Restart cost vs. height budget). Lemma C.56 and Proposition C.55 together
show a quantitative mismatch between what a BF excursion earns and what re-entry costs. A
depth-k excursion gains 2k + 3 bits of height excess; but each re-entry target Bk consumes
6k + 5 bits of arithmetic precision in the parameter q. In particular:

   · For k = 1: gain = 5 bits, cost of B1 re-entry = 11 bits (deficit 6 bits);

                                                            186
   · For k = 4: gain = 11 bits, cost of B1 re-entry = 11 bits (break-even); cost of B2 re-entry
       = 17 bits (deficit 6);

   · For all k: cost of re-entry into Bk with k  2 exceeds the gain by  4k + 2 - 2k bits.

The height budget never permits the orbit to "accumulate depth": each excursion's gain is
at best sufficient to restart at the same depth (and only for k  4), while deeper re-entry is
exponentially suppressed. This provides the quantitative mechanism behind Conjecture C.53.

Remark C.60 (Status of Conjecture C.53). Conjecture C.53 is the pointwise lift of Theo-
rem C.52, and is logically equivalent to the deterministic version of (C-2adic). Its current
status:

  (a) Haar-a.e. true. Theorem C.52 (this subsection) and Theorem C.24 both establish the
       conjecture for µ-almost every n  Z2.

  (b) Empirically true with margin. Among the 39 140 deterministic samples of Corollary C.25,
       every observed K(n) is finite (in fact every observed orbit halts at 1 in  5.04(k + log2 n)
       odd steps).

  (c) Pointwise on N open. A deterministic proof on the Haar-null subset N  Z2 requires
       either an effective Borel­Cantelli sieve (route (b) of Remark C.49) or a pointwise ergodic
       theorem on N  Bk (route (a)).

The conjecture is therefore stronger than what Theorem C.52 proves and weaker than the full
Collatz conjecture: it asserts only that the BF growth mechanism is not infinitely reusable along
any single N-orbit.

Remark C.61 (What would follow from Conjecture C.53). If Conjecture C.53 holds, then
for every n  N there is a maximum depth K(n) <  beyond which the orbit visits no BF
cylinder. After the last visit to BK(n) the orbit is permanently outside every Bk, k > K(n), and
Layer B's empirical generic-drift observation (-0.332 bits/step on the post-escape suffix of all
8 BF champions) becomes the relevant dynamics. Combined with the unconditional Layer A
density-(1 - n-15) closure, this would upgrade the global picture to: every orbit halts after a
uniformly bounded amount of cycle-tracking growth, plus a generic-drift collapse to 1. We do
not assert this implication as a theorem, because we do not have Conjecture C.53; we only state
what its proof would buy.

Remark C.62 (Comparison with the GPT-contributed sketch). A draft post-escape irre-
versibility argument circulated during the v6 revision proposed to deduce non-restartability
directly from the exponential decay of µ(Bk) in k. That draft is substantively the content of
Theorem C.52: it proves the Haar-a.e. statement via Borel­Cantelli on the 2-adic measure. The
deterministic step "exact reconstruction for arbitrarily large k is not dynamically sustainable
by a single orbit" is, however, the conclusion sought rather than something derived; it coincides
with Conjecture C.53 above. We have therefore retained the reframing (non-restartability is a
sharper formulation of the residual obstruction) and promoted the Haar-a.e. half to a proved
theorem, while keeping the deterministic half explicit as conjectural. The conjecture is strictly
weaker than the full Collatz conjecture but strictly stronger than what current ergodic methods
deliver.

C.10 Density-1 cylinder boundedness via union bound

We now upgrade Theorem C.52 from the Haar setting to a natural-density statement on N. The
argument uses only (i) the finite-modulus structure of Bk, (ii) the measure-preserving property
of T2 on Z2, and (iii) the density-1 stopping-time bound of Theorem 9.144. The result is strictly

                                                            187
weaker than Conjecture C.53 (it allows the maximum cylinder depth K(n) to grow like log log n
rather than be uniformly bounded) but strictly stronger than the Haar-only form: it puts the
obstruction inside an explicit, slowly growing window.

Lemma C.63 (Preimage density). For each k  1 and each j  0, the preimage T2-j(Bk)  Z2
is a finite union of clopen balls, and its natural density on N equals its Haar measure on Z2,
namely 2-(6k+8).

Proof. T2 is the unique continuous extension of the odd-Collatz step to Z2, and is Haar-measure-
preserving by Lagarias [10]. The set Bk is the clopen ball {x  nk (mod 26k+8)}. For j  1,

                                  T2-j(Bk) =                Rv1,...,vj ,

                                              (v1,...,vj )

where each Rv1,...,vj is the residue class of starting points whose first j valuation-pattern is
(v1, . . . , vj) and whose j-th iterate lies in Bk. Each Rv1,...,vj is itself a residue class modulo

26k+8+v1+···+vj , hence has natural density 2-(6k+8+v1+···+vj) on N. Summing over the (countable)
valuation patterns and using (v1,...,vj) 2-(v1+···+vj) = 1 gives total density 2-(6k+8), as claimed.
Measure preservation of T2 on (Z2, µ) verifies the same identity at the Haar level.

Theorem C.64 (Density-1 cylinder boundedness). Assume the conclusion of Theorem 9.144:

for natural-density-1 starting points n  N, the Collatz stopping time satisfies  (n)  Cd log2 n
for some absolute constant Cd. Let K(N ) := (1 + )(log2 log2 N )/6 for any fixed  > 0. Then

                 1   n  N :  j  0,  k > K(N ), T2j(n)  Bk                 ---- 0.

Equivalently, for natural-density-1 n  N, the re-entry signature K(n) of Definition C.51 satis-

fies sup K(n)    1+  log2 log2 n  for  al l  sufficiently  large  n.

Corollary C.65 (Quantitative slow growth of K(n)). Conditional on the same density-1
stopping-time bound, the re-entry depth function K(n) := sup K(n) satisfies K(n) = O(log log n)
on a natural-density-1 subset of N. In particular, the cylinder-cofinal set {n  N : K(n) = }
has natural density 0.

                                              188
Proof. Apply Theorem C.64 with any  > 0 along a sequence Nm  . The bound K(n) 
(1 + ) log2 log2 n/6 holds for all n  NNm, and m NNm has natural density 1 as a countable

Remark C.66 (Status and what is missing). Theorem C.64 closes the natural-density half of
Conjecture C.53: it shows that the cylinder-cofinal set of N has natural density 0 and, moreover,
that the maximum cylinder depth grows at most double-logarithmically on a density-1 subset.
This is the N-version of Theorem C.52 and the sharpest density-side statement compatible with
the present methods.

    What it does not establish is the deterministic statement that sup K(n) <  for every
n  N. The residual obstruction is the same density-0 exceptional set that obstructs every
Collatz density-1 result, specifically the set on which the Cramér ladder of Theorem 9.144 fails.
Closing it would require an effective discrepancy bound for the orbit's visit frequency to dyadic
cylinders, or a pointwise ergodic theorem on the sparse subset N  Bk.

    The conditional dependence on Theorem 9.144 is essential: without it, the inner sum J(N )
in the union bound cannot be bounded by O(log N ) on density 1 - o(1), and the geometric tail
does not beat the linear count.

An unconditional starting-point density bound

The conditional dependence above is on the orbit length. If we restrict the question to n that
begin in a deep cylinder (rather than n whose orbit later visits one), we obtain an unconditional
companion theorem.

Theorem C.67 (Unconditional starting-point cylinder density). For each K  1, the natural
density of

                                 SK := { n  N : n  Bk for some k > K }

on N exists and equals

d(SK )                  =                    =  2-(6K+8)  ·  2-6  =  2-(6K+14)
                                                1 - 2-6               1 - 2-6 .
                                   2-(6k+8)

                           k=K +1

In particular d(SK)  (64/63) · 2-6K-14, decaying geometrically in K, with no hypothesis on
stopping times. For K = 0 this gives d( k1 Bk) = 2-8 · 2-6/(1 - 2-6)  6.10 · 10-5, in

agreement with the empirical fraction of starting points hitting Bk measured below.

Proof. The sets Bk are pairwise disjoint, since membership in Bk requires the orbit to escape
the Karp cycle after exactly k laps and an orbit cannot escape at two different lap counts
simultaneously. Each Bk is the residue class {n  nk (mod 26k+8)}, hence has exact natural
density 2-(6k+8) on N. Summing the disjoint geometric series gives the stated value. No

hypothesis beyond the definition of Bk is used.

Remark C.68 (Why the orbit-visit upgrade requires a stopping-time hypothesis). Theo-
rem C.67 is unconditional but weaker than Theorem C.64: it counts n that start in a deep
Bk, not n whose orbit visits one. Upgrading from start to orbit visits requires multiplying the
per-cylinder density by the number of orbit iterates that fall into each cylinder; the cleanest
such bound uses J(n)  C log2 n, which is exactly the content of Theorem 9.144. The route
via Theorem C.6 is also blocked: the prefix tail controls hitting time of I2 (cylinder-averaged)
but not the total stopping time  , because once an orbit hits I2 it can re-emerge into the tran-
sient region multiple times, and bounding the number of re-emergences pointwise is the same
difficulty as bounding  directly. We therefore have the following unconditional / conditional
split:

· Unconditional (Theorem C.67): density of n  SK is  1.07 · 2-6K-14.

                                             189
· Conditional on Theorem 9.144 (Theorem C.64): density of n whose orbit ever visits
      k>K Bk is  C log2 N/26K , vanishing for K  log2 log2 N .

The gap between "starts in SK" and "orbit ever visits SK" is therefore the same as the con-
ditional / unconditional gap on the average stopping time over n  N ­ a classical Collatz
question.

Remark C.69 (Effective range from Brina verification). For n  268, the Brina computa-
tional verification [2] furnishes a uniform bound  (n)  T0 with T0  2 000 (the actual maximum
stopping time in the verified range). Substituting this into the proof of Theorem C.64 gives an
unconditional version on the verified range:

1  {n  N  : j, k > K,  T2j(n)  Bk}      T0 · 2-(6K+13) · (1 + o(1))  for  N  268.
N

With K = 4 this gives a density bound of  T0 ·2-37  2000·7.3·10-12  1.5·10-8, deterministic
and computable. The Brina range therefore deterministically rules out a positive density of
cylinder visits at depth K  4 for all n  268.

Corollary C.70 (Empirical K(n) distribution, 5 × 106 odd n). Let K(n) := sup K(n) be the

deepest BF cylinder visited by the forward orbit of n (0 if no cylinder is visited). Sweeping all
odd n  [1, 107] produces the distribution

                       K #{n : K(n) = K} fraction

                       0  4 978 337         0.99567

                       1  20 907            0.00418

                       2  752               0.00015

                       3            4       8×10-7

          4                         0       < 2×10-7

The empirical maximum Km(eamxp)(n  107) = 3, attained first at n = 4 107 423 with log2 n 
21.97. The fraction of odd n  N whose orbit ever visits any Bk is 0.433%, about 70× the natural
density of k Bk itself (6.10 · 10-5), the amplification representing the average number of orbit
positions per starting n. The observed values are consistent with the conditional Theorem C.64:
the density-o(1) exceptional set is non-empty but very sparse (4 orbits in 5 × 106 exceed K = 2),
and the empirical fraction of orbits with K(n) > 0 matches the unconditional Theorem C.67's
prediction  70 · d( Bk) within a factor close to the average orbit length over n  107.

Proof. Computational. The script k_sweep.py computes the forward orbit of each odd n  107,
tests membership of each iterate T2j(n) in Bk for 1  k  14 via the single-mod-comparison
T2j(n)  nk (mod 26k+8), and records K(n) = max{k : some iterate is in Bk}. The orbit is
computed in exact integer arithmetic. Total runtime is  10 minutes on a single core.

Remark C.71 (Empirical first-appearance scaling of Kmax). The first appearance of K = 1, 2, 3
occurs at n = 4 575, 53 247, 4 107 423 respectively, with log2 n values 12.16, 15.70, 21.97. The
gap between consecutive "first appearances" is roughly 6 bits, matching the bit-width 6 of one

Karp lap (i.e. the additive log-cost of upgrading from Bk to Bk+1). Extrapolating, K = 4 should
first appear near log2 n  28 (n  2.7 · 108); K = 5 near log2 n  34 (n  1.7 · 1010); and so
on. This  6-bit-per-level rare-tail scaling is consistent with Kmax(n)  (1/6) log2 n along the
rarest tail of the K-distribution, while the bulk of the distribution still respects the conditional

ceiling Kpred(n)  (1/6) log2 log2 n. The two scalings are not in tension: the first describes the
density-o(1) exceptional tail; the second describes the density-1 bulk. The fact that even the

rare tail grows as O(log n) rather than as a fixed constant is precisely the quantitative content

of the Haar/pointwise gap.

                                       190
C.11 A tensor-rank law for the joint 2-adic/3-adic Syracuse transition oper-
          ator

The results of Section C.10 and the preceding remarks view the Syracuse operator one marginal
at a time: either purely 2-adically (Lagarias [10]) or purely 3-adically (Tao [14]). In this
subsection we record an empirical scaling law for the joint 2-adic/3-adic one-step transition
operator that is exact at machine precision on every configuration we have tested.

Definition C.72 (Joint Syracuse transition tensor). Fix integers a  3 and b  2, and write
M2 = 2a, M3 = 3b, D = M2M3. The joint Syracuse transition tensor

                                              P (a,b)  RM2×M3×M2×M3

is defined by

P (a,b)[i, j, i, j] = Pr T (n)  (i, j) (mod (M2, M3)) n  (i, j) (mod (M2, M3)) ,

where T is the Syracuse map T (n) = (3n + 1)/2v2(3n+1) on odd integers. Its matricized rank
Rtrue(a, b) is the rank of the D × D reshape of P (a,b) grouping input modes (i, j) into rows and
output modes (i, j) into columns.

Proposition C.73 (Empirical rank law, verified on 15 configurations). For every (a, b) tested
with a  {3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8} and b  {2, 3, 4} such that D  5184, the matricized rank of the joint
Syracuse transition tensor satisfies

               Rtrue(a, b) = 3b 2a-3 + 1 .                                                   (61)

Equivalently, the compression ratio is

               Rtrue(a, b)              =             1  +  1   ---  1
                    D                                 8     2a       8.
                                                                a

Structural proof modulo Lemmas C.75 and C.76. Partition the odd residues (Z/2a)× into a generic

part G(a) := {i odd : v2(3i+1) < a} and a singleton exceptional part {i0} where i0 is the unique
odd residue mod 2a with 3i0 + 1  0 (mod 2a) (uniqueness: i0  -3-1 (mod 2a)).

    Step 1: generic rows are rank-one. Fix i  G(a) with k := v2(3i + 1)  {1, . . . , a - 1} and
set m(i) := (3i + 1)/2k, which is odd. For any n with n  i (mod 2a) and n  j (mod 3b),
writing n = i + 2as gives 3n + 1 = 2k m(i) + 3 · 2a-ks , and the second factor is odd because

m(i) is odd. Hence v2(3n + 1) = k exactly, and

T (n) mod 2a = m(i) + 3 · 2a-ks mod 2a, T (n) mod 3b = (3j + 1) · 2-k mod 3b.

As s varies the 2-adic image traces out the entire coset Ck(i) := m(i) + 2a-kZ/2a, uniformly
(since 3 is a unit mod 2k). Writing uCk(i) for the normalized indicator of Ck(i) in R2a and e
for the -th standard basis vector in R3b, the (i, j) row of P (a,b) is the rank-one vector

               P (a,b)[i, j, ·, ·] = uCk(i)  eck(j),        ck(j) := (3j + 1) · 2-k mod 3b.  (62)

    Step 2: 3-adic mod-3 class is determined by the parity of k. Because 22  1 (mod 3),
2-k mod 3 equals 2 if k is odd and 1 if k is even, so ck(j)  2-k (mod 3) is independent of j

and depends only on k mod 2. Consequently the outputs of odd-k generic rows lie in the coset
Lodd := {  Z/3b :   2 mod 3,   0 mod 3} of size 3b-1, while even-k generic outputs lie in
the disjoint coset Leven, also of size 3b-1.

    Step 3: 2-adic generic span. Define the level-k span Vk := span{uCk(i) : i  G(a), v2(3i+1) =
k}  R2a , and let V2odd := k odd Vk, V2even := k even Vk. We claim

               dim V2odd = 2a-2,                           dim V2even = 2a-3.

                                                      191
For k = 1: the condition v2(3i + 1) = 1 means i  3 (mod 4), giving i = 4t + 3 for t =
0, . . . , 2a-2 - 1, and m(i) = 6t + 5. As t varies, 6t + 5 mod 2a-1 hits every odd residue exactly
once (since gcd(6, 2a-1) = 2 and 6t covers every even residue mod 2a-1), so the 2a-2 level-1
cosets form a partition of odd residues mod 2a into blocks of size 2; their indicators are linearly
independent, giving dim V1 = 2a-2. Every level-k coset with k  2 is a union of 2k-1 level-1
cosets, so Vk  V1 for all k  1, and V2odd = V1 has dimension 2a-2. For k = 2: the condition
v2(3i + 1) = 2 is i  1 (mod 8) (one checks 3 · 1 + 1 = 4 with v2 = 2), giving i = 8s + 1 and
m(i) = 6s + 1. As s = 0, . . . , 2a-3 - 1, 6s + 1 mod 2a-2 hits 2a-3 distinct odd residues (same
argument), so level-2 cosets partition odd residues mod 2a into 2a-3 blocks of size 4. Hence
dim V2 = 2a-3, and every higher even level k  {4, 6, . . .} has cosets that are unions of level-2
cosets, so V2even = V2 has dimension 2a-3.

    Step 4: generic rank. By Steps 1­2 the generic row space splits as an internal direct sum

         span P (a,b)[i, j, ·, ·] : i  G(a), j  Z/3b = V2odd  Wodd  V2even  Weven ,

where Wodd := span{e :   Lodd} has dimension 3b-1, and similarly for Weven. The disjointness
of the summands follows from disjointness of Lodd and Leven in Z/3b. Step 3 gives

Rgen(a, b) = 3b-1 2a-2 + 2a-3 = 3b-1 · 3 · 2a-3 = 3b · 2a-3.

    Step 5: assembly. By Lemma C.75 below, the exceptional row block {P (a,b)[i0, j, ·, ·] : j 
Z/3b} has rank 3b. By Lemma C.76, the generic and exceptional row spans intersect trivially.
Therefore Rtrue(a, b) = Rgen(a, b) + 3b = 3b 2a-3 + 1 , which is equation (61).

Numerical verification. Steps 1­4 above are purely algebraic and hold for all a  3, b  2.

The residual content of Proposition C.73 beyond this is contained in Lemmas C.75 and C.76,
both of which are verified computationally below. For each (a, b) the tensor P (a,b) is built

by enumerating odd integers and normalizing each input row; the D × D matricization is
decomposed by SVD, and ranks are read at strict tolerance 10-10. All 15 measured full ranks
equal the predicted value, and in each case the generic block has rank 3b · 2a-3, the exceptional
block has rank 3b, and the two span a direct sum:

b=2             b=3            b=4

7 1152 153 153 7 3456 459 459
